q_{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
of hypermultiplet fields under
\mr{U(1)}_{u}
. Using the relation (
115
) we see that these covariant derivatives are local expressions for
\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{tw}}
on suitable associated
G^{\mr{tw}}
-vector bundles.
Moreover, since
F^{u}
is the curvature for the background
\mr{U(1)}_{u}
-connection
B^{u}_{\mu}
, we have

\left[\frac{F^{u}}{2\pi}\right]=c_{1}(u)~,\quad i=1,\ldots,\mr{rank}({\mathbb{T}}^{\mr{f}})~,

(129)

where
c_{1}(u)
is as in (
53
). It turns out that the action is
{\cal Q}
-exact up to topological terms:

\s{S}^{\mr{twisted}}_{{\cal N}=2+\mr{bck}}=\{\s{Q},V\}+\s{S}^{\mr{top}}~,

(130)

where

\displaystyle\mr{V}

\displaystyle:=\mr{V}_{\mr{vec}}+\mr{V}_{\mr{hyp}}~,

(131)

\displaystyle\mr{V}_{\mr{vec}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)~\mr{Im\,}\tau_{ab}\left(\frac{1}{4}\chi^{\mu\nu,a}\left(F_{\mu\nu}^{+}+D_{\mu\nu}\right)^{b}-\frac{1}{4}\eta^{a}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi}]^{b}+\frac{1}{2}\psi^{\mu,a}D_{\mu}\widetilde{\phi}^{b}\right)~,

(132)

\displaystyle\mr{V}_{\mr{hyp}}

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)\left(D_{\mu}\widetilde{M}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\alpha}^{\mu}\lambda^{\alpha}+D_{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\alpha}^{\mu}\mu^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}m\widetilde{\lambda}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{\alpha}h^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}m\widetilde{M}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\widetilde{\mu}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}\lambda^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{M}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\widetilde{\phi}\widetilde{\mu}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}+\widetilde{\lambda}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\widetilde{\phi}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\right)-\widetilde{M}^{(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\chi_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta})}\right)~.

(133)

Since
\mr{V}
and
{\cal S}^{\mr{top}}
are gauge invariant and invariant under the action of central charge (see Table
6
), it follows that on a closed
4
-manifold,

\left\{{\cal Q},\s{S}^{\mr{twisted}}_{{\cal N}=2+\mr{bck}}\right\}=0~.

(134)

The response currents to variations of the background metric and background
\mr{U(1)}
connections are
{\cal Q}
-exact as a result of
{\cal Q}
-exactness of the action. Thus the twisted partition function and topological correlators are formally locally constant as a function of the background connections.

7.1
Non-Abelian Monopole Equations

Given that the action is
{\cal Q}
-exact up to a topological term, one can show that the path integral localizes to supersymmetric backgrounds i.e., solutions of
{\cal Q}
-fixed equations with fermions set to zero. From (
113
), we see that supersymmetric configurations correspond to the solutions of the following
{\cal Q}
-fixed equations:

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{l}D_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}^{a}=F_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}^{a}~,\\
h_{\alpha u}=0~,\\
\widetilde{h}^{u}_{\alpha}=0~,\end{array}\right\}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}a=1,\ldots,\mr{dim}(G^{\mr{gauge}})~,\\
u=1,\ldots,\mr{rank}({\mathbb{T}}^{\mr{f}})~.\\
\end{array}

Setting the auxiliary fields of the dynamical vectormultiplets and hypermultiplets to their equations of motion

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{l}D_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}^{a}=2\widetilde{M}^{u}_{(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}T_{\varrho}^{a}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta})u}~,\\
h_{\alpha v}=\sigma_{\alpha\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}^{\mu}D_{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}_{v}~,\\
\widetilde{h}^{v}_{\alpha}=-D_{\mu}\widetilde{M}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}v}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\alpha}^{\mu}~,\end{array}\right\}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$u$ summed over}~,\\
v=1,\ldots,\mr{rank}({\mathbb{T}}^{\mr{f}})~,\\
\end{array}

yields

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{l}F_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}^{a}-2\widetilde{M}^{u}_{(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}T_{\varrho}^{a}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta})u}=0~,\\
\sigma_{\alpha\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}^{\mu}D_{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}_{v}=0~,\\
\sigma_{\alpha\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}^{\mu}D_{\mu}\widetilde{M}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}v}=0~,\end{array}\right\}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$u$ summed over}~,\\
v=1,\ldots,\mr{rank}({\mathbb{T}}^{\mr{f}})\\
\end{array}~.

(145)

These
{\cal Q}
-fixed equations are called
non-Abelian monopole equations

LMn (95)
;
HPP (95)
;
LNS (97)
;
PT (95)
;
Wit (99)

and are generalizations of both the usual ASD instanton equations and the “monopole” or “Seiberg-Witten” equations
Wit (94)
. We can write these equations in coordinate-free notation as follows: the monopole field
M
is a section of the associated
G^{\mr{tw}}
-vector bundle associated with the representation
(\bm{1},\bm{2},\mr{def},\varrho)
. Denote this vector bundle by
E^{\mr{mon}}
. The conjugate monopole field
\widetilde{M}
is a section of the associated
G^{\mr{tw}}
-vector bundle associated to the representation
(\bm{1},\bm{2},\overline{\mr{def}},\overline{\varrho})
. Denote this vector bundle by
\overline{E}^{\mr{mon}}
. On every local chart
(U,x^{\mu})
on
{\mathbb{X}}
, there is a natural bilinear map:

\begin{split}\mu:\Gamma(\overline{E}^{\mr{mon}})\times\Gamma(E^{\mr{mon}})&\longrightarrow\Omega^{2,+}(U,\mr{ad}\,P^{\mr{gauge}})\\
(\widetilde{M},M)&\longmapsto\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{~\underline{a}}_{\mu}e^{~\underline{b}}_{\nu}\widetilde{\sigma}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}_{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}}\widetilde{M}^{u}_{(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}T_{\varrho}^{a}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta})u}T^{a}\right)dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}~,\end{split}

(146)

where we have chosen a local section
s:U\to P^{\mr{tw}}
to write

\widetilde{M}=[(s,\widetilde{M}^{u}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}m})]~,\quad M=[(s,M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}u}^{\overline{n}})]~,

(147)

where
m
and
\overline{n}
are indices for the representations
\varrho
and
\overline{\varrho}
respectively. We have chosen a basis for the orthonormal frame bundle with vielbein
e_{\mu}{}^{\underline{a}}
in the local chart. Finally as noted before,
D_{\mu}
acting on
\widetilde{M}
and
M
is the Dirac operator coupled to
\overline{E}^{\mr{mon}}
and
E^{\mr{mon}}
respectively. The monopole equations can then be written as

F^{+}\left((p_{4})_{*}(\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{tw}})\right)=2\mu(\widetilde{M},M)~,\quad\not{D}_{E^{\mr{mon}}}M=0~,\quad\not{D}_{\overline{E}^{\mr{mon}}}\widetilde{M}=0~,

(148)

where
F^{+}\left((p_{4})_{*}(\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{tw}})\right)
is the SD part of the curvature of the
G^{\mr{gauge}}
-connection
(p_{4})_{*}(\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{tw}})
on
P^{\mr{gauge}}
and
\not{D}=\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mu}D_{\mu}
.
This allows us to generalize the non-Abelian monopole equations to any compact, simple gauge group.

The path integral for topological correlators and the partition function formally localizes to the moduli space of non-Abelian monopole equations. After performing the path integral, we obtain topological invariants which depend on the first Chern classes
c_{1}(u)
and the background fields
\rho_{u}(\mu(b))
for 1-form symmetries constrained according to (
53
). This topological dependence on the background fields arises from the topological terms
{\cal S}^{\mr{top}}
in the action.

In conclusion,
after integrating out the dynamical fields we obtain a twisted partition function depending only on the data
1
–
3
.

8
Topological Twisting Of General Class
{\cal S}
Theories

We now turn to class
{\cal S}
theories. In general, we cannot apply the general discussion of Sections
3
-
7
because in general class
{\cal S}
theories are non-Lagrangian.
(Theories of type
A_{1}
are Lagrangian, and this is a notable and important exception.)

8.1
Pants Decompositions

As noted in
GMN (09)
;
Gai (09)
weak coupling regimes are determined by a pants decomposition of the ultraviolet curve
C_{g,n}
used to define the theory. Here
g
is the genus and

n
the number of punctures.

Recall that a pants decomposition of a stable Riemann surface
C_{g,n}

Hat ()
is a maximal collection of pairwise disjoint simple closed curves (also called
cutting curves
or
cuffs
).
34
34

34

A simple closed curve on
{\mathbb{X}}
is a map
c:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{X}}
that is closed, i.e.,
c(0)=c(1)
, and also injective as a map
[0,1)\to{\mathbb{X}}
. Following
MM (21)
, we will refer to cutting curves surrounding the punctures as
external cutting curves
, and cutting curves
c_{i}
associated with gauge groups as
internal cutting curves
. In the mathematical literature, only the internal cutting curves are counted as
cuffs
.

For a given connected surface
C_{g,n}
, any pants decomposition will consist of
(2g-2+n)
pairs of pants.
35
35

35

The Euler characteristic
\chi(C_{g,n})=2-2g-n
of a stable Riemann surface is negative, this excludes
C_{0,0}
,
C_{0,1}
,
C_{0,2}
, and
C_{1,0}
.
Strictly speaking, one should distinguish between boundary components and punctures (the latter being marked points in the interior of the closure of the surface). Here the
n
“punctures” are obtained by removing
n
small open disks from the surface.

Moreover, any pants decomposition consists of
(3g-3+n)
internal cutting curves and
n
external cutting curves.
We denote a typical pants decomposition by a list of cutting curves:

\mf{p}=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{3g-3+2n}\}~.

(149)

The complement of these curves in
C_{g,n}
is a collection of
(2g-2+n)
pairs of pants
36
36

36

A pair of pants (hereafter abbreviated simply to “pants”) is a surface homeomorphic to
C_{0,3}
.
(also called trinions).

In these regimes we can express the theory as a gauge theory, coupled to possibly non-Lagrangian theories associated with the trinions. The key point is that we need only study general properties of the partition functions of these (possibly non-Lagrangian) trinion theories in the presence of background fields. This is all that is needed to discuss the analogues of the cohomological conditions
(
53
) and the twisting procedure.

8.2
Trinion Theories

The trinion theory
37
37

37

As noted in Section
9
, the trinion theory is a class
{\cal S}
theory on a three-punctured sphere. The notation
T[\mathfrak{g},D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}},D_{p_{3}}]
specifies the gauge algebra of the 6d theory and the puncture data
D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}},D_{p_{3}}
at the three punctures. The data at a regular puncture
p
includes a reductive Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{p}
and a homomorphism
\rho:\mf{su(2)}\to\mathfrak{g}_{p}
, among other things. The flavor symmetry algebra
\mathfrak{f}_{p}^{\mr{fl}}
at puncture
p
is given by the centralizer of the image of
\rho
in
\mathfrak{g}_{p}
. See
CDT (12)
;
Tac13b ()
;
Tac (15)
, for a more complete discussion of the punctures in class
{\cal S}
theories.

T[\mathfrak{g},D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}},D_{p_{3}}]
is a relative field theory. We would like to gauge a collection of trinion theories to produce a class
{\cal S}
theory, and we would like the latter to be an absolute 4d theory that can be defined on all standard four-manifolds. We will make the conservative assumption that this requires each component trinion theory to be an absolute theory definable on all standard four-manifolds.
38
38

38

It would be interesting if one could relax this conservative assumption by gauging the relative trinion theories to produce an absolute theory.

It is thought that the trinion theory can be defined as an absolute 4d theory once some extra data is specified

Tac13a ()
. For the trinion theory, it is argued in
Tac13a ()

that the only extra data is a choice of global form of the symmetry group. (Equivalently, a choice of global form of the group is used to define the Hitchin system.)
We simply denote this extra data as
{\cal L}
so the absolute theory is denoted
T[\mathfrak{g},D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}},D_{p_{3}};{\cal L}]
.

For our considerations, we need to know the global symmetry
group
of the theory, not just its Lie algebra. The group form of the symmetry group of
class
{\cal S}
theories in general, and trinion theories, in particular, has been studied in
Tac13a ()
;
ADZGEH (20)
;
Bha (21)
;
BHSN (21)
;
BGHSN (22)
;
GEHLS (21)
;
DZGE (22)
;
HLL+ (22)
;
G2̊ (3)
.
39
39

39

See also
BDZH (22)
;
BDZHK (22)
for some recent work on generalized global symmetries of class
{\cal S}
theories.

The viewpoint of this paper, based on remarks from section
2
,
differs slightly from the above references. We regard the global symmetry as a
choice
based on the choice of domain category in the functorial formulation of field theory.

To each puncture
p
we associate a global symmetry algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{\mr{fl}}
and we denote by
\widetilde{G_{p}}
a Lie group with this algebra which is a product of a simply connected semi-simple Lie group and a torus. (Specification of the torus requires further discussion, not found here.) We can then define a theory
T[\mathfrak{g},D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}},D_{p_{3}};{\cal L}]
with
symmetry group of the form

\widetilde{G}_{T}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\widetilde{G_{p_{1}}}\times\widetilde{G_{p_{1}}}\times\widetilde{G_{p_{1}}}~.

(150)

Since, as we have mentioned, the theories
T[\mathfrak{g},D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}},D_{p_{3}};{\cal L}]
are in general non-Lagrangian it is very useful to adopt the functorial viewpoint of what is meant by a “theory” as in section
2
. Thus we take a “theory” to be a functor

\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}:\mathsf{Bord}_{\leq 4}^{\widetilde{{\cal F}}}\to\mathsf{VECT}~,

(151)

where
\widetilde{{\cal F}}
is a set of background fields that includes the groupoid of principal
\widetilde{G}_{T}
bundles with connection.
We identify the
\mr{Spin(4)}
factor with the covering group of the tangent bundle of
{\mathbb{X}}
, and hence the bordism category
\mr{Bord}_{\leq 4}^{\widetilde{{\cal F}}}
only includes spin manifolds. The background fields
\widetilde{{\cal F}}
should include the full set of vectormultiplets for the flavor symmetry so the partition function
on
{\mathbb{X}}
in the presence of background fields for
\widetilde{G}_{T}
can be written
informally as:

\mr{Z}_{T}[{\mathbb{X}},\omega_{\mu}{}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}},A^{\mr{R}}_{\mu},\mr{VM}_{p_{1}},\mr{VM}_{p_{2}},\mr{VM}_{p_{3}}]~,

(152)

where
\mr{VM}_{p}
is the
full set
of fields of the standard
{\cal N}=2
vectormultiplet.

It has been noted in
Tac13a ()
;
Bha (21)

that the symmetry group of the trinion theory can be taken to be a quotient

G_{T}:=\widetilde{G}_{T}/C_{T}~,

(153)

where
C_{T}
is a subgroup of the center of the numerator.
As in (
8
) above we require that the functor
\widetilde{\mr{Z}}

factors through

\mr{Z}_{T}:\mathsf{Bord}_{4}^{{\cal F}}\to\mathsf{VECT}~.

(154)

For purposes of topological twisting, we would like this bordism category to include non-spin manifolds. Therefore, the subgroup
C_{T}
should
satisfy (
11
).
Applying the reasoning of section
2
we arrive at:

C_{T}\subset C_{T}^{\mr{max}}:=\{(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\mu):(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\mu)\cdot\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}=\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}\}\subset Z(\widetilde{G}_{T})~,

(155)

where
\mu\in Z(\widetilde{G_{p_{1}}}\times\widetilde{G_{p_{1}}}\times\widetilde{G_{p_{1}}})
.

Very little is known about the trinion theories. Therefore we will restrict attention to the top level of the functor, i.e., the partition function.
Fortunately, one example of a partition function is known, it is the superconformal index
40
40

40

See Appendix
E
for a brief review.

Rom (05)
;
KMMR (05)
;
GPRR (09)
;
GRRY11a ()
;
GRRY11b ()
;
Tac (15)
which is
\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}
evaluated on
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
with nonbounding spin structure on
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, coupled to a
\widetilde{G}_{T}
bundle with connections specialized so as to give an index.
We can work out the action of
C_{T}
on these. Given our present ignorance, we will simply

postulate
that if
C_{T}
acts trivially on the superconformal index it acts trivially on
\widetilde{\mr{Z}}
and therefore
\widetilde{\mr{Z}}
factorizes.
41
41

41

In fact, although this assumption seems to be standard in the literature, it has a fundamental flaw. It is well known that the action of a symmetry group on operators
O\to UOU^{\dagger}
differs slightly from that on states
|\psi\rangle\to U|\psi\rangle
. Put more formally, the group that acts faithfully on the Hilbert space is in general a central extension of the group that acts faithfully on the operator algebra. This happens quite frequently in quantum theory. Sure enough, in the case of class
{\cal S}
theories, L. Hollands and A. Neitzke
HN (16)
discovered that the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
central subgroup of
\mr{E_{6}}
acts
nontrivially
on the spaces of BPS states on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
in the Minahan-Nemeschansky theory. They show that the Hilbert space of BPS states has a nontrivial isotypical decomposition for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
action where the degeneracy spaces for all three irreps are infinite-dimensional, and has nontrivial BPS index. (The IR description of the degeneracy spaces has a
{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}
grading from an IR
\mr{U(1)}\times\mr{U(1)}
global symmetry.)
It is conceivable that, when working on compact manifolds only the adjoint group of
\mr{E_{6}}
acts faithfully on the Hilbert space, but we are not aware of any evidence for or against this. We will nevertheless proceed to determine
C_{T}^{\mr{max}}
from the superconformal index,
faute de mieux
, bearing in mind that our results might need to be refined by a central extension.

The full superconformal index is not known in closed analytic form for generic trinion theories but a simplification known as the Schur index is known (see Appendix
E
). The Schur index for a
d=4

{\cal N}=2
theory (on
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
) has the trace formula

{{\cal I}}_{\mr{Schur}}(q,\bm{g}):=\mr{Tr}_{{\cal H}({\mathbb{S}}^{3})}(-1)^{\mr{F}}q^{\Delta-\frac{R}{2}}\bm{g}~,

(156)

where
{\cal H}({\mathbb{S}}^{3})
is the Hilbert space of the theory on
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
,
\mr{F}
is the fermion number,
\Delta
is the dilatation generator,
R
is the Cartan generator of
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
, and
\bm{g}\in G^{\mr{f}}
is the fugacity for the flavor symmetry (understood as an operator via its action on
{\cal H}({\mathbb{S}}^{3})
). Only the operators with quantum numbers satisfying

\Delta=R+\frac{j_{1}+j_{2}}{2}~,

(157)

contribute to the Schur index (these are the so-called Schur operators
GRRY11a ()
;
GRRY11b ()
;
RR (14)
;
RR (16)
), where
(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
in (
157
) are the eigenvalues of the Cartan generators of the
\mr{Spin(4)}\cong\mr{SU(2)}\times\mr{SU(2)}
isometry group of
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.
42
42

42

We use the same symbols for the operators and their eigenvalues.

Thus the Schur index (
156
) reduces to

{{\cal I}}_{\mr{Schur}}(q,\bm{g})=\mr{Tr}_{{\cal H}({\mathbb{S}}^{3})}(-1)^{\mr{F}}q^{\frac{R+j_{1}+j_{2}}{2}}\bm{g}~.

(158)

The fugacities are holonomies for background gauge fields. Thus an element
(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\mu)\in Z(\widetilde{G}_{T})
acts on the holonomies
q^{1/2}
and
\bm{g}
for the background gauge fields:
the holonomies simply get multiplied by
a
and
\mu
. From the exponent of
q^{1/2}
in (
158
), it is clear that the center acts on the fugacities as

\displaystyle(q^{\frac{1}{2}},\bm{g})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\zeta q^{\frac{1}{2}},\mu\bm{g})~.

(159)

In this way, we can use the action of the center on the Schur index to determine
C_{T}^{\mr{max}}
. We now work out two explicit examples.

{ex}

The simplest example is that of the trinion theory of class
{\cal S}
of type
A_{N-1}
with two “full” punctures and one “simple” puncture, so the global symmetry Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{u}(N)\oplus\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{u}(N)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
.
In this case

\widetilde{G}_{T}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}~.

(160)

This is a Lagrangian theory expressible in terms of free hypermultiplets and background vectormultiplets. We refer the reader to Appendix
F
for our notation, and relevant details of the action and the partition function. Here we just make use of the superconformal
index. The Schur index of the trinion theory with two “full” punctures and one “simple” puncture is given by
GRRY11a ()
;
Tac (15)

\displaystyle{{\cal I}}^{\mr{ffs}}_{\mr{Schur}}(q;\bm{a},\bm{b},\alpha)

\displaystyle=\prod_{u,i=1}^{N}\prod_{n\geq 0}\left[1-q^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{\alpha a_{i}}{b_{u}}\right)\right]^{-1}\cdot\left[1-q^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{b_{u}}{\alpha a_{i}}\right)\right]^{-1}~,

(161)

where
\bm{a},\bm{b}\in{\mathbb{T}}(\mr{SU(N)})
are the fugacities for the Cartan of the two
\mr{SU(N)}
flavor symmetries, parametrized as
\bm{a}=\mr{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
and
\bm{b}=\mr{diag}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})
, and
\alpha\in\mr{U(1)}
is the flavor fugacity for
\mr{U(1)}
.
Under the action of the central element

(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}}\textbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\textbf{1}_{N},\xi)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\times\mr{U(1)}~,

(162)

where
\omega:=e^{\frac{2\pi\mathsf{i}}{N}}
and
k_{1},k_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
, the fugacities transform as in (
159
):

q^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto\zeta q^{\frac{1}{2}}~,\quad(\bm{a},\bm{b},\alpha)\mapsto(\omega^{k_{1}}\bm{a},\omega^{k_{2}}\bm{b},\alpha\xi)~.

(163)

From the explicit character expansions of (
161
), we find that the Schur index is invariant under (
163
) if and only if

\zeta\omega^{k_{1}}\omega^{-k_{2}}\xi=1~,

(164)

and hence:

C_{T}^{\mr{max}}=\{(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\zeta\omega^{-k_{1}}\omega^{k_{2}})\in Z(\widetilde{G}_{T})\}~.

(165)

Clearly,

C_{T}^{\mr{max}}=\langle(-1,-1,-1,\textbf{1}_{N},\textbf{1}_{N},-1),(1,1,1,\omega\textbf{1}_{N},\textbf{1}_{N},\omega^{-1}),(1,1,1,\textbf{1}_{N},\omega\textbf{1}_{N},\omega)\rangle~.

(166)

There are different subgroups of
C_{T}^{\mr{max}}
satisfying (
155
). They define
different theories.

Topological Twisting Of The
\mr{ffs}
Theory.

To define a topological twisting we can take
\widetilde{G}^{\mr{tw}}:=\mr{Spin(4)}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{\mr{f}}
with
a homomorphism
\widetilde{G}^{\mr{tw}}\to\widetilde{G}_{T}
given by
(u_{1},u_{2},\mu)\mapsto((u_{1},u_{2}),u_{2},\mu)
which will descend to a
homomorphism
G^{\mr{tw}}\to G_{T}
for groups
C_{T}
described above, where
G^{\mr{tw}}\cong\widetilde{G}^{\mr{tw}}/C^{\mr{tw}}
and we can take
C^{\mr{tw}}
to be the kernel of the homomorphism from
\widetilde{G}^{\mr{tw}}
.
Via pullback under the homomorphism
G^{\mr{tw}}\to G_{T}
, the
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
-connection is identified with the self-dual part of the spin connection.

As explained in Section
7
, the bosonic scalars of the hypermultiplet become chiral spinors upon twisting. For details of the untwisted and twisted actions of the
\mr{ffs}
theory and our notation, see Appendix
F
. The Gaussian path integral over the twisted bosonic spinor fields yields the product of a topological invariant of the background fields and an inverse of the determinant of the square of the coupled Dirac operator. In particular, due to
{\cal Q}
-symmetry, the determinants cancel:

\begin{split}\int[{\cal D}\mr{HM}]e^{-S^{\mr{twisted}}_{\mr{hyp}}}=\left(\mr{Det}\left(\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\right)\right)^{-1}\mr{Det}\big(\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\big)\mr{Det}\big(-\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\big)=1~,\end{split}

(167)

and the partition function is simply given by a topological invariant of the background
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
bundle with connection:

\mr{Z}_{T}^{\mr{twisted}}=\exp\left(-\frac{\mr{i}}{16\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\tau_{ab}~(F^{a}\wedge F^{b})\right)~.

(168)

{ex}

We also consider the
A_{N-1}
type trinion theory with 3 “full” punctures (
\mr{fff}
) for which
43
43

43

There are some issues about whether one should use
\mr{SU(N)}
or
\mr{U(N)}
. We will not attempt to address them here.

\widetilde{G}_{T}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}~.

(169)

The only further information known about the operator content comes from the superconformal index
Rom (05)
;
KMMR (05)
(see Appendix
E
for a review). The Schur index of the
T_{N}
theory has the following form
GRRY11a ()
;
Tac (15)
:

\mathcal{I}_{\mr{Schur}}^{T_{N}}(q;\bm{a},\bm{b},\bm{c})=\frac{K(\bm{a})K(\bm{b})K(\bm{c})}{K_{0}}\sum_{\lambda}\frac{\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{a})\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{b})\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{c})}{\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{q^{\rho}})}~.

(170)

Here, the sum is over representations of the global flavor symmetries, for which the flavor symmetry elements are

(\bm{a},\bm{b},\bm{c})\in{\mathbb{T}}(\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)})~,

(171)

where
{\mathbb{T}}
denotes the standard maximal torus
and
\chi_{\lambda}
is the character of the representation
\lambda
.
Also,

\bm{q^{\rho}}:=\mr{diag}(q^{(N-1)/2},q^{(N-3)/2},\ldots,q^{(1-N)/2})\in\mr{SU(N)}~.

(172)

The prefactor in (
170
) is the contribution of the flavor multiplets to the index for
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}
, where for any
\bm{\mr{a}}\in\{\bm{a},\bm{b},\bm{c}\}
,

\displaystyle K(\bm{\mr{a}})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{n\geq 0}\bigg[(1-q^{n+1})^{1-N}\prod_{i\neq j}\left(1-q^{n+1}\frac{\mr{a}_{i}}{\mr{a}_{j}}\right)^{-1}\bigg]~,

(173)

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{d=2}^{N}\prod_{n\geq 0}(1-q^{d+n})~.

(174)

and
\bm{\mr{a}}:=\mr{diag}(\mr{a}_{1},\ldots,\mr{a}_{N})\in{\mathbb{T}}(\mr{SU(N)})
.

The element

(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{3}}\mathbf{1}_{N})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}~,

(175)

where
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
, acts on the fugacities as in (
159
), which implies

\bm{q^{\rho}}\mapsto\zeta^{N-1}\bm{q^{\rho}}~,\quad(\bm{a},\bm{b},\bm{c})\mapsto(\omega^{k_{1}}\bm{a},\omega^{k_{2}}\bm{b},\omega^{k_{3}}\bm{c})~.

(176)

Consider the term in the Schur index corresponding to a representation
\lambda
of
N
-ality
N(\lambda)
. From the expression of the character
\chi_{\lambda}
in terms of Schur polynomials, we infer that

\frac{\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{a})\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{b})\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{c})}{\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{q^{\rho}})}\longmapsto\big(\zeta^{N-1}\omega^{k_{1}}\omega^{k_{2}}\omega^{k_{3}}\big)^{N(\lambda)}~\frac{\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{a})\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{b})\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{c})}{\chi_{\lambda}(\bm{q^{\rho}})}~.

(177)

Note that the prefactor
K(\bm{a})K(\bm{b})K(\bm{c})/K_{0}
is invariant under translation by the center.
Since representations of all
N
-alities appear in the sum, we conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptC_{T}^{\mr{max}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{3}}\mathbf{1}_{N})\in Z(\widetilde{G}_{T}):\zeta\omega^{k_{1}}\omega^{k_{2}}\omega^{k_{3}}=1\}&\text{for }N\text{ even}~,\\
\{(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{3}}\mathbf{1}_{N})\in Z(\widetilde{G}_{T}):\omega^{k_{1}}\omega^{k_{2}}\omega^{k_{3}}=1\}&\text{for }N\text{ odd}~.\end{array}\right.

We now return to the general trinion theory. Since the theory is supersymmetric, its partition function
\mr{Z}_{T}
should satisfy a set of supersymmetric Ward identities
analogous to those of the partition function of a hypermultiplet in the presence of external fields. In particular, upon choosing a homomorphism

\varphi:G^{\mr{tw,bck}}\to\widetilde{G}_{T}/C_{T}~,

(180)

such that the pullback of the supersymmetry operators contains a singlet we obtain a function of the topologically twisted
VM
’s and the metric which is – moreover –
{\cal Q}
-closed, and therefore metric independent.
We take

G^{\mr{tw,bck}}=(\mr{Spin(4)}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{\mr{f}})/C^{\mr{tw,bck}}~,

(181)

and as in equation (
18
) we require:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mr{tw,bck}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}_{T}/C_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mr{Spin(4)}/\langle(-1,-1)\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}})/\langle(-1,-1,-1)\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\mr{tw,bck}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}\times p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\mr{W}}}

(182)

The description of
G^{\mr{tw,bck}}
and
\varphi^{\mr{tw,bck}}
is now very similar to that of the Lagrangian case. For example, for the
A_{N-1}

\mr{fff}
trinion theory, we can take

G^{\mr{tw,bck}}=(\mr{Spin(4)}\times{\mathbb{T}}(\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}))/C~,

(183)

C=\{(\zeta,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{3}}\mathbf{1}_{N})~|~\zeta^{N-1}\omega^{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}=1\}~,

(184)

and

\varphi^{\mr{tw,bck}}([(u_{1},u_{2}),\mu])=[(u_{1},u_{2}),u_{2},\mu]~.

(185)

8.3
Gaiotto Gluing

We are now ready to discuss the general class
{\cal S}
theory. In a weak-coupling domain, these can be assembled from trinion theories and vectormultiplets by the Gaiotto gluing conjecture
Gai (09)
. Recall that this states the following:

Consider two class
{\cal S}
theories

T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(1)},D^{(1)}]
and
T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(2)},D^{(2)}]
on (stable) Riemann surfaces
C^{(1)}
and
C^{(2)}
, with full punctures
D_{p_{1}}
in theory one,
D_{p_{2}}
in theory two (see footnote
37
). Then, since
\widetilde{G}_{p_{1}}=\widetilde{G}_{p_{2}}
we can gauge the diagonal subgroup

\widetilde{G}
of
\widetilde{G}_{p_{1}}\times\widetilde{G}_{p_{2}}
with coupling constant
\tau
. (When gluing full punctures the beta function vanishes so the coupling is scale-independent.) Denote the new gauged theory by

T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(1)},D^{(1)}]\times_{\tau}T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(2)},D^{(2)}]~.

(186)

On the other hand, we can glue the Riemann surfaces
C^{(1)}
to
C^{(2)}
with disks around
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
using the plumbing fixture
z_{1}z_{2}=q
. Denote the resulting Riemann surface
C^{(1)}\times_{p_{1},p_{2},q}C^{(2)}
. The Gaiotto gluing conjecture states that

T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(1)},D^{(1)}]\times_{\tau}T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(2)},D^{(2)}]=T[\mathfrak{g},C^{(1)}\times_{p_{1},p_{2},q}C^{(2)},D^{(1,2)}]~,

(187)

where
q=e^{2\pi\mathsf{i}\tau}
and
D^{(1,2)}
is the disjoint union of the punctures that were not glued.

{remark}

One may wonder if one could gauge a quotient of
\widetilde{G}_{p}
in the gluing procedure.
44
44

44

We thank Y. Tachikawa for some interesting remarks on this point.

Moreover, one may ask how the data making the theory associated with the glued surface absolute, is related to such data for the two factors. These are interesting and nontrivial questions we must leave for another time.

8.4
Expressing The Theory As A Gauge Theory

Now consider a pants decomposition of
C_{g,n}
into a set
{\cal T}
of trinions. We let
p_{i}
denote the external punctures, where
i
runs over the set of external punctures. The internal punctures come in unordered pairs

\{p_{\alpha,1},p_{\alpha,2}\}
with
\widetilde{G}_{p_{\alpha,1}}\cong\widetilde{G}_{p_{\alpha,2}}

where
\alpha
runs over the set of internal cutting curves.
Consider first the product of all the trinion theories associated to
T\in{\cal T}
. This will have a global symmetry group based on

\widetilde{G}_{{\cal T}}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\prod_{i}\widetilde{G}_{p_{i}}\times\prod_{\alpha}\left(\widetilde{G}_{p_{\alpha,1}}\times\widetilde{G}_{p_{\alpha,2}}\right)~.

(188)

The possible theories we can construct from this product of trinion theories will have global symmetry

\widetilde{G}_{{\cal T}}/C_{\cal T}
. Again, if we wish our domain category to include non-spin manifolds we use the considerations of section

2
to conclude that

(p_{1}\times p_{2})(C_{\cal T})=\langle(-1,-1,-1)\rangle\subset Z(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}})~.

(189)

as well as

C_{{\cal T}}\subset C_{{\cal T}}^{\mr{max}}:=\{(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,z)~|~(\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,z)\cdot\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}=\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}\quad\forall\,\,T\in{\cal T}\}~.

(190)

where
z
summarizes the component in the factors other than
\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
.

{ex}

Suppose, for simplicity, that all the punctures are “full” punctures.
For a given trinion
T\in{\cal T}
let
R_{T}(z)
be the
N^{th}
root of unity obtained by taking
the product of the
\mr{SU(N)}
factors in
z
pertaining to
T
. Then we require that
\zeta R_{T}(z)=1

for all
T\in{\cal T}
when
N
is even and
R_{T}(z)=1
for all
T\in{\cal T}
when
N
is odd.

Now we wish to gauge the diagonal flavor symmetry associated with the cutting curves.
For each
\alpha
consider the homomorphism
45
45

45

Other choices of gauge group are possible
CD (10)
. We restrict to the simplest case.

\psi_{\mr{glue}}:\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\to\widetilde{G}_{{\cal T}}~,

(191)

defined by

\psi_{\mr{glue}}:\prod_{\alpha}g_{\alpha}\longmapsto\bigg(\mathds{1}_{\mr{Spin(4)}},\mathds{1}_{\mr{SU(2)_{R}}},\prod_{i}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{G}_{i}},\prod_{\alpha}(g_{\alpha},g_{\alpha})\bigg)~.

(192)

Let
\pi:\widetilde{G}_{{\cal T}}\rightarrow\widetilde{G}_{{\cal T}}/C^{\prime}_{{\cal T}}
be the projection where

C^{\prime}_{{\cal T}}=\biggl[\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\prod_{i}\widetilde{G}_{p_{i}}\times\prod_{\alpha}\left(\Delta(\widetilde{G}_{p_{\alpha}})\right)\biggr]\cap C_{\cal T}~,

(193)

where
\Delta(G)\subset G\times G
is the diagonal subgroup. Then
\pi\circ\psi_{\mr{glue}}
will have a nontrivial kernel
K\subset Z\big(\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\big)
. The gauge group associated with the cutting curves is

\bigg(\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\bigg)\bigg/K~.

(194)

This determines what ’t Hooft fluxes can be turned on, and what
’t Hooft fluxes must be summed over when computing physical quantities.

The analog of
G^{\mr{phys}}
from section
3
is now

G_{\mr{S}}=\bigg(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\prod_{i}\widetilde{G}_{p_{i}}\times\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\bigg)\bigg/C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}}~.

(195)

(where
C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}}
is the preimage of
C^{\prime}_{{\cal T}}
under the product over
\alpha
of the map
\Delta:\mr{SU(N)}\to\Delta(\mr{SU(N)})
).

{remark}

1.

As a simple example consider the gluing of two
A_{N-1}
trinion theories with
\mr{fff}
punctures to form a genus two surface. Choosing
C=C^{\mr{max}}_{T}
the gauge group of the theory is

\left(\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\right)\big/\{(\omega^{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{3}}\mathbf{1}_{N})|\omega^{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}=1\}~.

(196)

Note that
K
is not a direct product of the form
\prod_{\alpha}K_{\alpha}
.

2.

The existence of a principal
G_{\mr{S}}
bundle
P_{\mr{S}}\to{\mathbb{X}}
in terms of cohomology classes of the bundles associated with transfer of structure group for the projections on the factors will be based on conditions analogous to the conditions (
53
) we found above. Some explicit examples are
given in Appendix
G
.

3.

It is an interesting question whether all the theories constructed above for different choices of
C
have six-dimensional descriptions, and what the extra data are in those descriptions. This question becomes highly relevant when considering the action of
S
-duality. In section

9
we will assume that all the theories have a 6d origin, and therefore the mapping class groups permute them as an
S
-duality group.

Now we turn to the partition function of the theory
{\cal S}
on a manifold
{\mathbb{X}}
.
It will have the general form:

\hskip-14.45377pt\mr{Z}_{{\cal S}}[\omega_{\mu}{}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}},A_{\mu}^{\mr{R}},\mr{VM}_{i}]=\int\prod_{c_{\alpha}}[d\mr{VM}_{c_{\alpha}}]e^{-S_{\mr{VM}_{c_{\alpha}}}}\prod_{T}Z_{T}[\omega_{\mu}{}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}},A_{\mu}^{\mr{R}},\mr{VM}_{p_{T,1}},\mr{VM}_{p_{T,2}},\mr{VM}_{p_{T,3}}]~,

(197)

where the product over
c_{\alpha}
is over the internal cutting curves and the product over
T
is a product over the trinions, and the vectormultiplet path integrals are for the gauge group (
194
).
We choose the external
\mr{VM}_{i}
so that the mass terms are generic, the external fermions are zero, and the structure group is reduced to the maximal torus. However, for the internal cutting curves
c_{\alpha}
one must maintain the full
\mr{VM}
in writing
\mr{Z}_{T}
in the integrand of the path integral.

8.5
Topological Twisting

Having framed the discussion as above, topological twisting proceeds in a completely analogous fashion to what we have described in previous sections.
We choose a homomorphism
\varphi^{\mr{tw,bck}}
so that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mr{tw}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\mr{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mr{Spin(4)}/\langle(-1,-1)\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}})/\langle(-1,-1,-1)\rangle~.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\mr{tw,bck}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}\times p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\mr{W}}}

(198)

Much as in the previous discussion we can take

G^{\mr{tw}}=\bigg(\mr{Spin(4)}\times{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(\prod_{i}\widetilde{G}_{i}\bigg)\times\bigg(\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\bigg)\bigg)\bigg/C^{\mr{tw}}_{\mr{S}}~.

(199)

To find
C^{\mr{tw}}_{\mr{S}}
we begin with

\widetilde{G}^{\mr{tw}}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(\prod_{i}\widetilde{G}_{i}\bigg)\times\bigg(\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\bigg)~,

(200)

and consider
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mr{tw}}:\widetilde{G}^{\mr{tw}}\rightarrow G_{\mr{S}}
defined by

\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mr{tw}}((u_{1},u_{2}),\mu,g)):=[(u_{1},u_{2}),u_{2},\mu,g]~.

(201)

The homomorphism has a kernel, which we can take to be
C^{\mr{tw}}_{\mr{S}}
.

Upon topological twisting the actions
S_{\mr{VM}_{c_{\alpha}}}
will be precisely of the form
equation (
119
). After doing the path integral over the internal vectormultiplets the path integral will depend on background fields. From this, we derive

G^{\mr{tw,bck}}
. The path integral will be invariant under continuous deformation
of connection on the
G^{\mr{tw,bck}}
-bundle and therefore will only depend on the
topological data outlined in items
1
–
3
.

9
Twisting
\mf{a}_{1}
Class
\s{S}
Theories And S-duality

The ideas presented so far can be used to twist
\mf{a}_{1}
class
\s{S}
theories. The cohomological conditions
for the existence of
P^{\mr{phys}}
were presented in
MM (21)
. Since these theories are Lagrangian theories we can rederive the conditions of
MM (21)

using the discussion in section
4
.
46
46

46

In
MM (21)
these conditions were cited as conditions for
topological twisting. One of the points of this paper is that the cohomological conditions for the existence of
P^{\mr{phys}}
should be distinguished from topological twisting.

Then, in section
9.2
we will study how these conditions change under
S
-duality, using the fact that these are also class
{\cal S}
theories.

9.1
Cohomological Conditions For The
\mf{a}_{1}
Class
{\cal S}
Theory

The weak coupling descriptions of
T[\mf{a}_{1},C_{g,n}]
corresponding to different pants decompositions of
C_{g,n}
were studied in
Gai (09)
;
GMN (10)
;
DMO (09)
.
The gauge algebra of the theory is

\mf{g}^{\mr{gauge}}=\mr{Lie}(G^{\mr{gauge}})=\mf{su}(2)^{3g-3+n}
, and as before,
\widetilde{G}^{\mr{phys}}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times G^{\mr{f}}\times\widetilde{G}^{\mr{gauge}}
,
where

\widetilde{G}^{\mr{gauge}}\cong\mr{SU(2)}^{3g-3+n},\quad n=\mr{rank}(G^{\mr{f}})~.

(202)

We will assume masses are generic and there is a reduction of flavor structure group to its maximal torus so we choose the domain category so that
G^{\mr{f}}\cong\mr{U(1)}^{n}
.
Note that

Z\big(\widetilde{G}^{\mr{phys}}\big)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mr{U(1)}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3g-3+n}~.

(203)

The different global forms of the physical theory are then characterized by choices of
C^{\mr{phys}}\subset Z(\widetilde{G}^{\mr{phys}})
, subject to the requirements discussed in section
3
. There are several choices of
C^{\mr{phys}}
(see, for example,
(
GMN, 10
, Section 2.3.1)
for a related discussion).

We then identify

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{\mr{gauge}}\quad

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c_{\alpha}\\
\mr{internal}\end{subarray}}\mr{SU(2)}_{c_{\alpha}}~,\quad G^{\mr{f}}\quad=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c_{i}\\
\mr{external}\end{subarray}}\mr{U(1)}_{c_{i}}~.

(204)

We define the projection maps,

p_{4,c_{\alpha}}:\widetilde{G}^{\mr{phys}}\longrightarrow\mr{SU(2)}_{c_{\alpha}}~,\quad p_{3,c_{i}}:\widetilde{G}^{\mr{phys}}\longrightarrow\mr{U(1)}_{c_{i}}~,

(205)

to the
c_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{th}}
and
c_{i}^{\mathrm{th}}
factors respectively. The preliminary discussion so far has been for the physical
\mf{a}_{1}
class
{\cal S}
theory on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
(after a Wick rotation from
{\mathbb{R}}^{1,3}
). Next, we seek cohomological conditions – guided by the discussion of Section
4
– from the existence of the bundle
P^{\mr{phys}}\to{\mathbb{X}}
, for the
\mf{a}_{1}
class
{\cal S}
theory on the 4-manifold
{\mathbb{X}}
.

As discussed above, we choose
C^{\mr{phys}}
so that the field theory functor factors through
a functor from a bordism category that includes non-spin manifolds. As before, this leads to

w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=w_{2}(P^{\mr{R}})~.

(206)

To derive additional conditions, we first note that
p_{4}(C^{\mr{phys}})\cong C^{\mr{grb}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3g-3+n}
,
so the characteristic class
\mu(b)\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},C^{\mr{grb}})
of the
C^{\mr{grb}}
-gerbe
b
can then be projected to a tuple of characteristic classes
\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{X}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)
, one for each internal cutting curve
c_{\alpha}
, by projecting
C^{\mr{phys}}
onto the
c_{\alpha}^{th}
factor using (
205
).
47
47

47

Explicitly, let
\varpi
be the map
\varpi:H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},C^{\mr{grb}})\to H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3g-3+n})
induced by the inclusion
C^{\mr{grb}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3g-3+n}
. Therefore
\varpi(\mu(b))
is a
(3g-3+n)
-tuple of classes in
H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. Then
p_{4,c_{\alpha}}
induces a natural projection onto the
c_{\alpha}^{th}
factor of this tuple, which we denote by
\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. If
\mu(b)\in\mathsf{ker\,}\varpi
, then the corresponding
\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}=0
for all
c_{\alpha}
.

Note that, depending on
our choice of
C^{\mr{phys}}
, which determines
C^{\mr{grb}}
, it might happen that
\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}
vanishes. We also recall remark
4
above: one should include a topological boundary condition
\rho
for the 5d gerbe
b
which determines which ’t Hooft fluxes are to be summed over.

The matter content of the theory is a collection of half-hypermultiplets
\Phi_{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}

labeled by unordered triples of cutting curves of the pants decomposition.
48
48

48

Therefore, the scalar fields in
\Phi_{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}
are in a real subspace
of the
2_{c_{1}}\otimes 2_{c_{2}}\otimes 2_{c_{3}}\otimes 2_{\mr{SU(2)_{R}}}
giving a total of
16 independent real scalar fields.

Let us begin by considering conditions associated with
\Phi_{c_{i}c_{j}c_{\alpha}}
, containing
one internal and two external punctures.
Introduce the notation

P^{\mr{f},c_{i}}=(p_{3,c_{i}})_{*}(P^{\mr{phys}})~.

(207)

Then
P^{\mr{f},c_{i}}
is a
\mr{U(1)}/p_{3,c_{i}}(C^{\mr{phys}})
-bundle. Let
c_{1}(c_{i})
denote the first Chern class of the
\mr{U(1)}
-bundle obtained by transfer of structure group of
P^{\mr{f},c_{i}}
along the isomorphism (see footnote
23
)
\mr{U(1)}/p_{3,c_{i}}(C^{\mr{phys}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mr{U}(1)
.
Now, item
2
of Section
4
(or (
53
)) directly yields the conditions

\displaystyle c_{1}\left(c_{i}\right)+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

(208)

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{j})+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

Next, we consider trinions
\Phi_{c_{i},c_{\alpha},c_{\beta}}
bounded by one external cutting curve
c_{i}
and two internal cutting curves
c_{\alpha}
,
c_{\beta}
.
Following Section
4
, we obtain

c_{1}(c_{i})+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\beta}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

(209)

Finally for trinions
\Phi_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}c_{\gamma}}
bounded by three internal cutting curves
c_{\alpha}
,
c_{\beta}
,
c_{\gamma}
, the matter
\Phi_{c_{\alpha}c_{\beta}c_{\gamma}}
is a half-hypermultiplet in the
\bm{2}_{\alpha}\otimes\bm{2}_{\beta}\otimes\bm{2}_{\gamma}
of the
\mr{SU(2)}_{c_{\alpha}}\times\mr{SU(2)}_{c_{\beta}}\times\mr{SU(2)}_{c_{\gamma}}
factor of
\widetilde{G}
. The discussion in Section
4
leads to the cohomological condition

\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\beta}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\gamma}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

(210)

9.2
Transformation Of Cohomological Conditions Under S-duality

We next analyze the effect of S-duality transformations on these cohomological conditions. Recall that the S-duality group of the theory is the mapping class group
\mr{MCG}(C_{g,n})
and acts on the space of gauge couplings of the theory (identified with the Teichmüller space of
C_{g,n}
, see
Wit (97)
;
GMN (09)
;
Gai (09)
).
\mr{MCG}(C_{g,n})
, being the group of isotopy classes of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of
C_{g,n}
, acts on the set of simple closed curves on
C_{g,n}
, and thus also acts on the (infinite) set of all pants decompositions of
C_{g,n}
. The set of equivalence classes of pants decompositions modulo the action of the mapping class group
\mr{MCG}(C_{g,n})
is finite.
49
49

49

Note that
\mr{MCG}(C_{g,n})
is isomorphic to the automorphism group of the
pants graph
or
pants complex
of
C_{g,n}
, see
Mar (04)
for a proof. This is the pants complex of Hatcher and Thurston
HT (80)
with the inclusion of 2-cells by Hatcher, Lochak, and Schneps
HLS (00)
. The resulting pants complex is connected and simply connected.
\mr{MCG}(C_{g,n})
acts co-compactly on this complex
Wol (09)
.

Thus, there are finitely many S-duality orbits of theories based on
C_{g,n}
. We will show that there is a natural transformation of the cohomological conditions of theories in the same S-duality orbit. To do so, we introduce the
graph
of a pants decomposition of
C_{g,n}
.

Given a pants decomposition
\mf{p}
, we define an undirected graph
G(\mf{p})
of
\mf{p}
with vertices as the pairs of pants
and
external cutting curves in
\mf{p}
, such that two pairs of pants are connected by an edge in
G(\mf{p})
if they are bounded by a common internal cutting curve. Further, an external cutting curve (a vertex in
G(\mf{p})
) is connected by an edge in
G(\mf{p})
to that pair of pants which it bounds (also a vertex in
G(\mf{p})
). Thus
G(\mf{p})
has
2g-2+n
trivalent vertices,
n
univalent vertices and
3g-3+2n
edges. An illustrative example is shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
The graph of a pants decomposition of
C_{2,2}
. Figure adapted from
Wol (09)
.

Two pants decompositions
\mf{p}
,
\mf{p}^{\prime}
are in the same
\mr{MCG}(C_{g,n})
-orbit if and only if the corresponding graphs
G(\mf{p})
,
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
are isomorphic
Wol (09)
.
50
50

50

Two graphs
G(\mf{p})
and
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
are said to be isomorphic if there exists a bijection between their vertex sets
f:V\big(G(\mf{p}))\to V\big(G(\mf{p}^{\prime})\big)
such that any two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in V\big(G(\mf{p})\big)
of
G(\mf{p})
are adjacent in
G(\mf{p})
if and only if their images
f(v_{1})
and
f(v_{2})
are adjacent in
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
. When such an isomorphism exists between
G(\mf{p})
and
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
, we will frequently write it as
f:G(\mf{p})\to G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
, with the understanding that it is an isomorphism between the corresponding vertex sets.

We can now describe the cohomological conditions for the theory defined by the pants decomposition
\mf{p}
in terms of the graph
G(\mf{p})
as follows: we begin by labeling the vertices and edges in
G(\mf{p})
as

\displaystyle V\big(G(\mf{p})\big):=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{2g-2+n},u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\}~,

(211)

\displaystyle E\big(G(\mf{p})\big):=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{3g-3+n},\widetilde{e}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{e}_{n}\}~,

(212)

where
t
and
u
indicate trivalent and univalent vertices respectively, whereas the edge
e_{\alpha}
connects two trivalent vertices and
\widetilde{e}_{i}
connects a univalent vertex with a trivalent vertex.

In section
9.1
, we encountered maps
c_{i}\mapsto c_{1}(c_{i})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})
and
c_{\alpha}\mapsto\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. By design, the set of external cutting curves
\{c_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is bijectively mapped to the subset
\{\widetilde{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of edges of
G(\mf{p})
. Likewise, the set of internal cutting curves
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{3g-3+n}
also gets bijectively mapped to the subset
\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{3g-3+n}
. Note, however, that in each case, there might be several bijections due to different choices of indexing. We simply pick one, so that we have one-to-one map between elements of the sets
\{c_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\widetilde{e}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, and between elements of the sets
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{3g-3+n}
and
\{e_{\beta}\}_{\beta=1}^{3g-3+n}
.

Then, with this choice of map, we can associate fluxes to the edges in
G(\mf{p})
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{e}_{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto c_{1}(\widetilde{e}_{i})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})\quad\,\,\,\,\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,n~,

(213)

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mu(b)_{e_{\alpha}}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\quad\text{ for }\alpha=1,\ldots,3g-3+n~,

(214)

with the proviso that
\mu(b)_{e_{\alpha}}=0
if
e_{\alpha}
is a loop (i.e., an edge connecting one vertex to itself).
Then, the cohomological conditions described in the preceding section can be recast into equations at each trivalent vertex. If a trivalent vertex is connected by three edges
e_{\alpha}
,
e_{\beta}
,
e_{\gamma}
to three other trivalent vertices, then we impose the condition

\mu(b)_{e_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{e_{\beta}}+\mu(b)_{e_{\gamma}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0~.

(215)

If a trivalent vertex is connected by edges
e_{\alpha},e_{\beta},\widetilde{e}_{i}
to two trivalent and one univalent vertex, then we have the cohomological condition

c_{1}(\widetilde{e}_{i})+\mu(b)_{e_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{e_{\beta}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

(216)

Finally, if a trivalent vertex is connected by edges
e_{\alpha}
,
\widetilde{e}_{i}
,
\widetilde{e}_{j}
to a trivalent and two univalent vertices, then

\displaystyle c_{1}(\widetilde{e}_{i})+\mu(b)_{e_{\alpha}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

(217)

\displaystyle c_{1}(\widetilde{e}_{j})+\mu(b)_{e_{\alpha}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

(218)

Now consider two pants decompositions
\mf{p}
,
\mf{p}^{\prime}
with graphs
G(\mf{p})
,
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
in the same
\mr{MCG}(\Sigma_{g,n})
-orbit.
Let the vertices and edges of
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
be labeled as

\displaystyle V\big(G(\mf{p}^{\prime})\big):=\{t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{2g-2+n},u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{n}\}~,

(219)

\displaystyle E\big(G(\mf{p}^{\prime})\big):=\{e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{3g-3+n},\widetilde{e}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{e}^{\prime}_{n}\}~.

(220)

We associate fluxes to the edges as in (
213
) and (
214
).

Recall that for an undirected graph
G(\mf{p})
, its line graph
L(G(\mf{p}))
is constructed in the following way: to each edge in
G(\mf{p})
, we assign a vertex in
L(G(\mf{p}))
51
51

51

Therefore,
V\big(L(G(\mf{p}))\big)\cong E\big(G(\mf{p})\big)
.

and for every two edges in
G(\mf{p})
emanating from a common vertex, we assign an edge between their corresponding vertices in
L(G(\mf{p}))
. Since
G(\mf{p})\cong G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
, by the Whitney graph isomorphism theorem
(
Har, 69
, Theorem 8.3)
, their line graphs
are also isomorphic:

L(G(\mf{p}))\cong L(G(\mf{p}^{\prime}))~.

(221)

Moreover, there is a one-to-one correspondence between graph isomorphisms
G(\mf{p})\cong G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
and the corresponding line graph isomorphisms
L(G(\mf{p}))\cong L(G(\mf{p}^{\prime}))
.
52
52

52

The Whitney graph isomorphism theorem implies an equivalence between isomorphisms of graphs and isomorphisms of corresponding line graphs with the following exception: the graphs
K_{3}
(the complete graph on three vertices, a.k.a. the three-vertex triangle) and
K_{1,3}
(a complete bipartite graph, a.k.a. the four-vertex claw) are non-isomorphic but their line graphs are isomorphic. (See
(
Har, 69
, Ch. 8)
for a discussion of this point.) However, this does not concern us for two reasons: first of all, we require only the forward implication of the theorem which says that
if
two graphs are isomorphic,
then
their corresponding line graphs are isomorphic, and second, the graphs
K_{3}
and
K_{1,3}
cannot arise from any pants decomposition.

The vertices of the graphs
L(G(\mf{p}))
and
L(G(\mf{p}^{\prime}))
are respectively,
E\big(G(\mf{p})\big)
and
E\big(G(\mf{p}^{\prime})\big)
. Choose an isomorphism
\bm{F}:E\big(G(\mf{p})\big)\to E\big(G(\mf{p}^{\prime})\big)
and let
f:G(\mf{p})\to G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
denote the graph isomorphism corresponding to
\bm{F}
.

Let us denote an edge in
G(\mf{p})
connecting two vertices
u
and
v
by
uv
.
By arguments outlined in
(
Har, 69
, Theorem 8.3)
, we have

\bm{F}(uv)=f(u)f(v)~.

(222)

In particular, if
e_{\alpha}
,
e_{\beta}
, and
e_{\gamma}
are edges in
G(\mf{p})
connecting a vertex
u
to three other
trivalent
vertices, then
\bm{F}(e_{\alpha})
,
\bm{F}(e_{\beta})
,
\bm{F}(e_{\gamma})
are three edges in
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
connecting
f(u)
to three other
trivalent
vertices in
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
. Thus by (
215
), the cohomological condition at the trivalent vertex
f(u)
in
G(\mf{p}^{\prime})
is

\mu(b^{\prime})_{\bm{F}(e_{\alpha})}+\mu(b^{\prime})_{\bm{F}(e_{\beta})}+\mu(b^{\prime})_{\bm{F}(e_{\gamma})}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0~,

(223)

where
b^{\prime}
is the gerbe for the theory defined by the pants decomposition
\mf{p}^{\prime}
.
Arguing along the same lines, we conclude that the cohomological conditions for the theory defined by
\mf{p}^{\prime}
are given by applying the map

\widetilde{e}_{i}\longmapsto\bm{F}(\widetilde{e}_{i})~,\quad e_{\alpha}\longmapsto\bm{F}(e_{\alpha})~,

(224)

to (
215
), (
216
), (
217
) and (
218
).
Thus, we see that at least in this class of examples,
S
-dual theories with different Lagrangians require precisely the same cohomological conditions as needed for the existence of
P^{\mr{phys}}
.

We emphasize that in general the cohomological conditions for two Lagrangian theories which are not in the same S-duality orbit are different and
not
related by a nice transformation. Consider, for example, the
\mr{a}_{1}
class
{\cal S}
theory on a 3 punctured torus
C_{1,3}
. Consider the two pants decompositions of
C_{1,3}
shown in Figure
2
below.

Figure 2
:
Two
\mr{MCG}(C_{1,3})
-inequivalent pants decompositions of
C_{1,3}
and their associated pants graphs.

The pants graphs are non-isomorphic as can be easily checked using their adjacency matrix and hence their corresponding Lagrangian theories are
not
related by
S
-duality. Let us now look at the cohomological conditions we get for the two theories using the general prescription described above. There are three pairs of pants in both pants decompositions. With the labeling of cutting curves in Figure
2
, we obtain the following cohomological conditions for the two theories:

1.

For the pants decomposition
\mf{p}_{1}(C_{1,3})
we get

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{i})+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})

\displaystyle=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

(225)

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{k})+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})

\displaystyle=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

\displaystyle\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\beta}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\gamma}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})

\displaystyle=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{j})+\mu(b)_{c_{\beta}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\gamma}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})

\displaystyle=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

2.

For the pants decomposition
\mf{p}_{2}(C_{1,3})
we get

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{i})+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\beta}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

(226)

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{k})+\mu(b)_{c_{\beta}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\gamma}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~,

\displaystyle c_{1}(c_{j})+\mu(b)_{c_{\alpha}}+\mu(b)_{c_{\gamma}}+w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

We see that in the second case, we have no restriction on background gerbes since we can always choose
a suitable
c_{1}(c_{i,j,k})
while in the first case, the background gerbes are restricted. Thus the cohomological conditions for the two theories are different.

To twist these theories, we need to provide
C^{\mr{tw}}
and a homomorphism
\varphi^{\mr{tw}}:G^{\mr{tw}}\to G^{\mr{phys}}
satisfying (
18
).
Since these theories are Lagrangian, we simply
choose
C^{\mr{tw}}
as in
(
97
) above,
and define

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mr{tw}}:G^{\mr{tw}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G^{\mr{phys}}

(227)

\displaystyle{\left[\left(\left(u_{1},u_{2}\right),\mu,g\right)\right]}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left[\left(\left(u_{1},u_{2}\right),u_{2},\mu,g\right)\right].

The theories in different
S
-duality orbits correspond to different topological field theories.
