Appendix B
Transfer Of Structure Group

In this appendix, we recall the notion of transfer of structure group.
For simplicity, we will work in the category of smooth manifolds (where the objects are smooth manifolds and morphisms are smooth maps). Let
G
be a Lie group and
{\mathbb{X}}
a smooth manifold. Let
{\cal C}(G,{\mathbb{X}})
be the groupoid of principal
G
-bundles over
{\mathbb{X}}
. Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be two Lie groups. Given a homomorphism
\phi:G_{1}\longrightarrow G_{2}
, we have a functor
\phi_{*}:{\cal C}\left(G_{1},{\mathbb{X}}\right)\longrightarrow{\cal C}\left(G_{2},{\mathbb{X}}\right)
that maps a principal
G_{1}
-bundle
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
to
P_{1}\times_{\phi}G_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
where

P_{1}\times_{\phi}G_{2}:=\left(P_{1}\times G_{2}\right)/\sim_{\phi}~,

(228)

with equivalence relation is defined by

(p_{1}\cdot h,g)\sim_{\phi}\left(p_{1},\phi(h)g\right),\quad(p_{1},g)\in P_{1}\times G_{2},h\in G_{1}~.

(229)

The projection
\pi^{\prime}_{1}:P_{1}\times_{\phi}G_{2}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{X}}
is defined as
\pi^{\prime}_{1}([(p_{1},g)])=\pi_{1}(p_{1})
and the right
G_{2}
action is given by

[(p_{1},g_{1})]\cdot g_{2}=\left[\left(p_{1},g_{1}\cdot g_{2}\right)\right]~.

(230)

Let
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
be a principal
G_{1}
-bundle. The
transfer of structure group
of
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
using the homomorphism

\phi:G_{1}\longrightarrow G_{2}
is, by definition, the image
\phi_{*}(P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}})
under the above functor. One can show that in terms of patches and transition functions, this means that
if the local trivialization of
P_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}{\mathbb{X}}
is
given by
\{U_{p},t_{pq}\}
, then the local trivialization of
\phi_{*}(P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}})
is given by
\{U_{p},\widetilde{t}_{pq}\}
with

\widetilde{t}_{pq}=\phi\circ t_{pq}
.

A related notion is a
reduction of structure group
defined as follows: let
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
be a principal
G_{2}
-bundle. A reduction of structure group of
P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
from
G_{2}
to
G_{1}
using the homomorphism

\phi:G_{1}\longrightarrow G_{2}
is a principal
G_{1}
-bundle

P_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}{\mathbb{X}}
together with an isomorphism of
\phi_{*}(P_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}{\mathbb{X}})
with
P_{2}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}{\mathbb{X}}
. One can show that in terms of patches and transition functions, this means that
there is an atlas and local trivialization of
P_{2}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}{\mathbb{X}}

given by
\{U_{p},\widetilde{t}_{pq}\}
such that there exists a
G_{1}
bundle

P_{1}\to{\mathbb{X}}
with local trivializations
\{U_{p},t_{pq}\}
such that

\widetilde{t}_{pq}=\phi\circ t_{pq}
.

It is worth noting that there is never an obstruction to transferring the structure group of a principal bundle, but there are, in general, nontrivial obstructions to the existence of a reduction of structure group.
Here are some examples of reductions of structure groups:

Fix a positive integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
. Given an
n
-manifold
{\mathbb{X}}
and a structure group
G
, a
G
-structure
Che (66)
on
{\mathbb{X}}
is a principal
G
-subbundle of the frame bundle
F{\mathbb{X}}
. Cases where the
G
-structure corresponds to standard tensor fields are familiar. For example, an
\mr{O(n)}
structure defines a Riemannian metric on
{\mathbb{X}}
and can be viewed as a principal
\mr{O(n)}
subbundle of the
\mr{GL(n,{\mathbb{R}})}
frame bundle, or equivalently, a reduction of structure group from
\mr{GL(n,{\mathbb{R}})}
to
\mr{O(n)}
via the natural inclusion. A further reduction to
\mr{SO(n)}
structure defines an orientation
53
53

53

An orientation
without
a choice of Riemannian metric corresponds to a reduction from
\mr{GL(n,{\mathbb{R}})}
to
\mr{GL^{+}(n,{\mathbb{R}})}
, the subgroup of matrices of positive determinant. A volume form corresponds to a reduction from
\mr{GL(n,{\mathbb{R}})}
to
\mr{SL(n,{\mathbb{R}})}
, the subgroup of matrices of determinant
+1
.

on
{\mathbb{X}}
, and a reduction to
\mr{Spin(n)}
via the standard homomorphism
\mr{Spin(n)}\to\mr{O(n)}
defines a spin structure. Indeed, some of these reductions might not exist – obstructions are quantified by certain characteristic classes. The aforementioned examples are of tangential structures, i.e., structure group reductions for the frame bundle. However, the idea of the reduction of structure group applies more generally to principal bundles.

The notion of transfer of structure group can be extended to include the data of a connection. Let
{\cal C}^{\mr{conn}}(G,{\mathbb{X}})
be the groupoid of principal
G
-bundles with connection. If we view a connection as a rule for the coherent lifting of paths then a
connection
\bm{\nabla}_{1}
on
P_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
lifts a path

\gamma:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{X}}
to a path
\widetilde{\gamma}:[0,1]\to P_{1}
, uniquely specified
once we are given the initial point
\widetilde{\gamma}(0)
in the fiber above
\gamma(0)
.
The image connection
\phi_{*}(\bm{\nabla}_{1})
is simply the connection on

P_{1}\times_{\phi}G_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{X}}
given by the rule to
lift the path
\gamma(t)
to
[(\widetilde{\gamma}(t),1)]
given the initial point

[(\widetilde{\gamma}(0),1)]
.

In terms of local patches and transition functions
\{U_{p},t_{pq}\}
for
P_{1}
we have
locally-defined
\mr{Lie}(G_{1})
-valued one-forms
A_{p}
, then
\phi_{*}(P_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}{\mathbb{X}})

has local trivializations
\left\{U_{p},\phi\circ t_{pq}\right\}
and the pushed-forward
connection
\phi_{*}(\bm{\nabla}_{1})
is represented by the locally-defined

\mr{Lie}(G_{2})
-valued one-forms
\phi_{*}(A_{p})
where

\phi_{*}:\mr{Lie}(G_{1})\to\mr{Lie}(G_{2})
is the induced morphism of Lie algebras.

Appendix C
Generalized Spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
Structure

In this section, we elaborate on the notion of generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structures on smooth, oriented 4-manifolds and give some examples. Our conventions and notation for Čech cohomology are from Section
4
. Define the generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
group

\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)=\frac{\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}}{\mr{C}}~,

(231)

where
\mr{C}
is a
finite
subgroup of the center:

\mr{C}\subset Z(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d})\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}~,

(232)

such that

\displaystyle p_{1}(\mr{C})

\displaystyle=\langle(-1,-1)\rangle\subset Z(\mr{SU(2)\times SU(2)})=Z(\mr{Spin}(4))~.

(233)

This ensures that

\displaystyle p_{1}(\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4))

\displaystyle\cong\mr{Spin(4)}/p_{1}(\mr{C})\cong\mr{SO(4)}~.

(234)

We define a principal
\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}}^{d}(4)
-bundle
P\to{\mathbb{X}}
to be
a generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structure if

p_{1,*}(P)\cong\mr{OFr}(T{\mathbb{X}})~.

(235)

A connection
\bm{\nabla}
on a principal
\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}}^{d}(4)
-bundle
P
is called a generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
connection if
p_{1,*}(P)(\bm{\nabla})\cong\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{LC}}
. A generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structure is thus a particular case of a
G
- structure on a 4-manifold with
G=\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
.
54
54

54

Reference
BI (23)
discusses “
Spin_{G}(4)
-structures,” which can be given a similar interpretation. It is possible that these are relevant to special topological twistings with nongeneric masses. See also reference
AM (20)
for other generalizations of spin
{}^{\mr{c}}
such as
\mr{Spin}^{\mr{h}}(n):=(\mr{Spin(n)}\times\mr{Sp(1)})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and
\mr{Spin}^{k}(n):=(\mr{Spin}(n)\times\mr{Spin}(k))/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
in
n
-dimensions (and any positive integer
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
). Reference
AI (80)
is an interesting early work on generalizations of spin structures, motivated by quantum gravity.

AI (80)
considers a reduction of structure group of the tangent bundle to
\mr{Spin}_{G}(4):=(\mr{Spin(4)}\times G)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
where
G
is a Lie group with center
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

Below, we present some examples to illustrate that a generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structure is in general different from the standard spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structure.
The latter exists on all oriented four-manifolds.
55
55

55

This follows from the important fact that on such manifolds, any class
w_{2}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
admits an integral lift, due to the existence of a characteristic element. This is equivalent to the vanishing of
W_{3}=\mathsf{Bock}(w_{2})
where
\mathsf{Bock}:H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to H^{3}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})
is the Bockstein map at degree
2
, associated with the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\times 2}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\,mod\,}2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\longrightarrow 0
. See
TV (94)
;
GS (99)
;
Sco (05)
for details.

{ex}

Consider the subgroup

\mr{C}=\langle(-1,-1,-1,\ldots,-1)\rangle=\mr{diag}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{d+2})\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}~.

(236)

We first show that for this choice of
\mr{C}
, a
\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}}^{d}(4)
-structure exists on any orientable 4-manifold, and is equivalent to a choice of
d
independent spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structures.

We start with transition functions

t_{pq}^{\mr{SO(4)}}:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{SO(4)}~,

(237)

for
\mr{OFr}({\mathbb{X}})
, and choose local lifts

\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-}\right):U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{SU(2)\times\mr{SU(2)}}\cong\mr{Spin(4)}~,

(238)

such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{pq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Spin(4)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{SO(4)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{t}^{+}_{pq},\widetilde{t}^{-}_{pq})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{pq}^{\mathsf{SO(4)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(239)

commutes, where
\pi:\mr{Spin(4)}\to\mr{SO(4)}
is the projection map. The
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-valued cochain

\displaystyle\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{\pm}\widetilde{t}_{qr}^{\pm}\widetilde{t}_{rp}^{\pm}~,

(240)

is a Čech representative of the Stiefel-Whitney class of
T{\mathbb{X}}
:

\left[\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}\right]:=w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},\mathbb{Z}_{2})~.

(241)

Choose
d
integral lifts of
w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})
, i.e.,
d
integer classes
\{c_{1}(u)\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})\}_{u=1}^{d}
such that

c_{1}(u)\equiv w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})\mathsf{\,mod\,}2~.

(242)

(Such integral lifts always exist – see footnote
55
.)
There exist complex line bundles
\{{\cal L}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{d}
with first Chern classes
\{c_{1}(u)\}_{u=1}^{d}
. Endowing each
{\cal L}_{u}
with a Hermitian metric on its fibers, we can regard
{\cal L}_{u}
as a
\mr{U(1)}
-bundle. Let us choose transition functions functions
(h_{u})_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
on
{\cal L}_{u}
. We further choose local lifts
(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{pq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U(1)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{u})_{pq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}

(243)

commutes, where
P_{2}:\mr{U(1)}\to\mr{U(1)}
is the squaring map, that acts as
z\mapsto z^{2}
for
z\in\mr{U(1)}
. Therefore,

(\eta_{u})_{pqr}:=(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{qr}(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{rp}:U_{pqr}\longrightarrow\mr{{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mr{U(1)}~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~,

(244)

represents the
\mathsf{\,mod\,}~2
reduction of
c_{1}(u)
:

\left[(\eta_{u})_{pqr}\right]=r_{2}(c_{1}(u))\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~,

(245)

or equivalently, the obstruction to finding a line bundle
{\cal W}_{u}
such that
{\cal W}_{u}^{\otimes 2}\cong{\cal L}_{u}
. Note that by definition, this
\mathsf{\,mod\,}~2
reduction is simply the second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})
due to (
242
). So the representatives
\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}
and
(\eta_{u})_{pqr}
are cohomologous in
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore, there exist coboundaries
(\varepsilon_{u})_{pqr}:U_{pqr}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
such that

\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}=(\varepsilon_{u})_{pqr}(\eta_{u})_{pqr}~.

(246)

Let
(\varepsilon_{u})_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
denote the trivializing maps of these coboundaries, that is,

(\varepsilon_{u})_{pqr}=(\varepsilon_{u})_{pq}(\varepsilon_{u})_{qr}(\varepsilon_{u})_{rp}~.

(247)

We define transition functions

\widetilde{t}_{pq}:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{Spin(4)\times U(1)}^{d}~,

(248)

via

\widetilde{t}_{pq}:=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},(\varepsilon_{1})_{pq}(\widetilde{h}_{1})_{pq},\cdots,(\varepsilon_{d})_{pq}(\widetilde{h}_{d})_{pq}\right)~.

(249)

Then (
246
) implies that

\widetilde{t}_{pq}\widetilde{t}_{qr}\widetilde{t}_{rp}=\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{+},\zeta_{pqr}^{-},(\varepsilon_{1})_{pqr}\left(\eta_{1}\right)_{pqr},\cdots,(\varepsilon_{d})_{pqr}\left(\eta_{d}\right)_{pqr}\right)\in\mr{C}~.

(250)

Thus the transition functions

t_{pq}:=[\widetilde{t}_{pq}]:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)~,

(251)

(where
[\cdot]
indicates an equivalence class due to the quotient by
\mr{C}
), satisfy the cocycle condition

t_{pq}(x)t_{qr}(x)t_{rp}(x)=\mathds{1}_{\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)}~.

(252)

Therefore, using these transition functions, we can construct a
\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
-bundle
P\to{\mathbb{X}}
. Finally, because of (
234
), the bundle
P
satisfies

(p_{1})_{*}(P)\cong\mr{OFr}({\mathbb{X}})~.

(253)

The defining data of a
\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
structure is a choice of
d
integral lifts of
w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})
. This is the same data needed to choose
d
spin
{}^{\mr{c}}
-structures. The groups
\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
and
(\mr{Spin}^{\mr{c}}(4))^{d}
are not isomorphic: they have distinct third homotopy groups:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi_{3}\big((\mr{Spin}^{\mr{c}}(4))^{d}\big)&\cong({\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}})^{\otimes d}~,\\
\pi_{3}\big(\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4))&\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}~.\end{split}

(254)

The groupoids of principal bundles based on these two structure groups are not isomorphic. However, once the latter bundles are subject to constraint (
235
), with a similar constraint
for each of the factors in
(\mr{Spin}^{\mr{c}}(4))^{d}
there will be a 1-1 correspondence. Let us elaborate on this.

An element of the set of
d
spin
{}^{\mr{c}}
-structures is a collection of equivalence classes of pairs of maps
\{[\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq},h^{i}_{pq}]\}_{i=1}^{d}
where
\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{Spin(4)}
and
h^{i}_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
. For a fixed
i
, both elements of this pair fail by a common
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-valued cochain. However, since the Čech 2-cocycle constructed out of any choice
56
56

56

The transition functions are determined up to possible bundle automorphisms (a.k.a. gauge transformations or frame rotations).

of
\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq}
is a representative of the second Stiefel-Whitney class of
T{\mathbb{X}}
, the only choice one has is in picking the transition functions
h^{i}_{pq}
. With this Čech data, we can define a map

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Set of $d$ spin${}^{\mr{c}}$ structures}&\longrightarrow\text{Set of $\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)$ structures)}\\
\big\{[\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq},h^{i}_{pq}]\big\}_{i=1}^{d}&\longmapsto[\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq},h^{1}_{pq},\ldots,h^{d}_{pq}]~,\end{split}

(255)

where we have picked – without loss of generality – one of the transition functions to
\mr{Spin(4)}
, namely the
i^{th}
one. We can also define another map

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Set of $\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)$ structures}&\longrightarrow\text{Set of $d$ spin${}^{\mr{c}}$ structures}\\
[\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq},h^{1}_{pq},\ldots,h^{d}_{pq}]&\longmapsto\big\{[\widetilde{t}^{\,i}_{pq},h^{j}_{pq}]\big\}_{j=1}^{d}~,\end{split}

(256)

The composition of the maps (
255
) and (
256
) clearly defines a one-to-one correspondence of the set of
d
spin
{}^{\mr{c}}
structures to itself. This example illustrates that it is not sufficient to say that the groups are not isomorphic to claim that this structure is different from a choice of
d
spin
{}^{\mr{c}}
structures.

{ex}

Consider a
finite
subgroup
\mr{C}
of the following subgroup of the center of
\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}
:

\mr{C}\subset\bigg\{\left(\zeta,\zeta,\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}~|~\zeta\prod_{k=1}^{d}\mu_{k}=1\bigg\}~.

(257)

Let us begin with transition functions

t_{pq}^{\mr{SO(4)}}:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{SO(4)}~,

(258)

for
\mr{OFr}({\mathbb{X}})
, and choose local lifts

\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-}\right):U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{SU(2)\times\mr{SU(2)}}\cong\mr{Spin(4)}~,

(259)

such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{pq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Spin(4)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{SO(4)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{t}^{+}_{pq},\widetilde{t}^{-}_{pq})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{pq}^{\mathsf{SO(4)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(260)

commutes, where
\pi:\mr{Spin(4)}\to\mr{SO(4)}
is the projection map. The
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-valued cochain

\displaystyle\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{\pm}\widetilde{t}_{qr}^{\pm}\widetilde{t}_{rp}^{\pm}~,

(261)

is a Čech representative of the Stiefel-Whitney class of
T{\mathbb{X}}
:

\left[\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}\right]:=w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},\mathbb{Z}_{2})~.

(262)

Note that
p_{u+1}(\mr{C})
(for any
u=1,\ldots,d
) is a cyclic group, since all finite subgroups of
\mr{U(1)}
are cyclic. Therefore, we define
d
integers
\{n_{u}\}_{u=1}^{d}
by

p_{u+1}(\mr{C})\cong\mathbb{Z}/n_{u}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mr{U(1)}~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~,

(263)

where
p_{u+1}(\mr{C})
denotes the projection onto the
(u+1)^{th}
factor of
\mr{C}
.

For each
\mr{U(1)}
factor in
\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}
, we introduce a complex line bundle
{\cal L}_{u}
with first Chern class
c_{1}(u)\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})
. At this point, this is a completely arbitrary choice, but we will momentarily present a cohomological constraint for the existence of a
\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}}^{d}(4)
-structure which will involve these Chern classes as well as the second Stiefel-Whitney class of
T{\mathbb{X}}
.

Let us endow all complex line bundles with Hermitian inner products on their fibers so that they become
\mr{U(1)}
bundles. Let
(h_{u})_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
be transition functions on the bundle
{\cal L}_{u}
. Also choose local lifts
(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{pq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U(1)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{u})_{pq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n_{u}}}

(264)

commutes, where
P_{n_{u}}:\mr{U(1)}\to\mr{U(1)}
is the
n_{u}^{th}
power map, that acts as
z\mapsto z^{n_{u}}
for
z\in\mr{U(1)}
. Then the cochain defined by

(\eta_{u})_{pqr}:=(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{qr}(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{rp}:U_{pqr}\longrightarrow\mr{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n_{u}{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mr{U(1)}~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~.

(265)

represents the
\mathsf{\,mod\,}~n_{u}
reduction of
c_{1}(u)
:

\left[(\eta_{u})_{pqr}\right]=r_{n_{u}}(c_{1}(u))\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/n_{u}{\mathbb{Z}})~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~.

(266)

For any fixed
u
, the class
\left[(\eta_{u})_{pqr}\right]
(or equivalently, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{u}}
-valued cochain
(\eta_{u})_{pqr}
) measures the obstruction to the existence of a globally-defined line bundle
{\cal W}_{u}
such that
{\cal W}_{u}^{\otimes n_{u}}\cong{\cal L}_{u}
.

At this point, we have
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-valued cochains
\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}
, and the
d
cochains of the form
(\eta_{u})_{pqr}
for
u=1,\ldots,d
, where, for a fixed
u
,
(\eta_{u})_{pqr}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{u}}
-valued.
57
57

57

It is instructive to contrast this with the Čech arguments that establish the existence of a standard spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
structure. There,
d=1
,
u=1
, and
n_{u}=2
. One has two
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-valued cochains, and due to the result of footnote
55
, they represent the
same

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
cohomology class, and must consequently differ by a coboundary.

Let us define

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle:=\mr{lcm}(2,n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})~.

(267)

Therefore,
N=2p
for some
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
. Hence, the maps

\displaystyle\rho_{s}:\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}~,

(268)

\displaystyle\iota_{u}:\mathbb{Z}/n_{u}\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~,

(269)

are well-defined. They induce the following maps in cohomology

\displaystyle\rho_{s}:H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}})~,

(270)

\displaystyle\iota_{u}:H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}/n_{u}{\mathbb{Z}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}})~,\quad u=1,\ldots,d~.

(271)

which, in general, are neither injective nor surjective.
58
58

58

For example, the induced map
\rho_{s}
on cohomology has a kernel isomorphic to the
p
-torsion submodule of
H^{\bullet}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})
, whereas the induced map
\iota_{u}
has a kernel isomorphic to the
(N/n_{u})
-torsion submodule of
H^{\bullet}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/n_{u}{\mathbb{Z}})
.

We define transition functions

\widetilde{t}_{pq}:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{Spin(4)\times U(1)}^{d}~,

(272)

by

\widetilde{t}_{pq}:=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},(\widetilde{h}_{1})_{pq},\cdots,(\widetilde{h}_{d})_{pq}\right)~.

(273)

With this definition,

\widetilde{t}_{pq}\widetilde{t}_{qr}\widetilde{t}_{rp}=\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{+},\zeta_{pqr}^{-},\left(\eta_{1}\right)_{pqr},\cdots,\left(\eta_{d}\right)_{pqr}\right)~.

(274)

It follows from the definition (
257
) that if

\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}\cdot\prod_{u=1}^{d}\left(\eta_{u}\right)_{pqr}=1~,

(275)

the transition functions
\widetilde{t}_{pq}
satisfy

\widetilde{t}_{pq}\widetilde{t}_{qr}\widetilde{t}_{rp}\in\mr{C}~,

(276)

as desired.
In particular, the transition functions

t_{pq}:=[\widetilde{t}_{pq}]:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)~,

(277)

(where
[\cdot]
indicates an equivalence class due to the quotient by
\mr{C}
), satisfy the cocycle condition

t_{pq}(x)t_{qr}(x)t_{rp}(x)=\mathds{1}_{\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)}~.

(278)

Then, following the same line of reasoning as in Section
4
, (
275
) implies the following cohomological condition:

\boxed{\rho_{s}\left(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})\right)+\sum_{u=1}^{d}\iota_{u}\circ r_{n_{u}}(c_{1}(u))=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}})~.}

(279)

We now argue that this cohomological condition enables us to construct explicitly a
\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}}^{d}(4)
cocycle. For this, we assume that a collection of line bundles
{\cal L}_{u}
exists with its first Chern classes chosen to satisfy (
279
).

The cohomological condition (
279
) implies that the product of the Čech cochains corresponding to the individual terms represents a coboundary:

\displaystyle\rho_{s}\big(\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}\big)\cdot\left[\prod_{u=1}^{d}\iota_{u}\big((\eta_{u})_{pqr}\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{pqr}:U_{pqr}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mr{U(1)}~.

(280)

Since
\varepsilon_{pqr}
is a coboundary, there exist trivializing maps

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{pq}:U_{pq}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mr{U(1)}~,

(281)

such that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{pqr}=\varepsilon_{pq}\varepsilon_{qr}\varepsilon_{rq}~.

(282)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho_{s}\big(\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}\big)\cdot\left[\prod_{u=1}^{d}\iota_{u}\big((\eta_{u})_{pqr}\big)\right]=\varepsilon_{pq}\varepsilon_{qr}\varepsilon_{rp}~.

(283)

Now, we can define the modified transition functions
59
59

59

By the
d^{th}
root of a generic phase
\varepsilon_{pq}
, we mean the following: we choose
a
solution
y
to the equation
y^{d}=\varepsilon_{pq}
and use it in (
284
)–(
286
). The conclusion (
287
) remains valid because the
d^{th}
power of
any
solution by definition equals
\varepsilon_{pq}
. So (
287
) is valid and independent of the branch chosen to define the
d^{th}
root. We could have avoided taking the
d^{th}
root – at the expense of an undemocratic treatment of the
\mr{U(1)}
bundles – by arbitrarily picking one of the
d

\mr{U(1)}
bundles – say the
u_{0}^{th}
one – and defining its modified transition functions as
(\widetilde{h}_{u_{0}})^{\prime}_{pq}:=(\widetilde{h}_{u_{0}})_{pq}\cdot(\varepsilon_{pq})^{-1}
, whereas
(\widetilde{h}_{u})^{\prime}_{pq}:=(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}
for
u=1,\ldots,d
,
u\neq u_{0}
.

\displaystyle(\widetilde{h}_{u})^{\prime}_{pq}:=(\widetilde{h}_{u})_{pq}\big(\varepsilon_{pq}\big)^{-1/d}~,

(284)

as well as maps
\widetilde{s}_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}
via

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{pq}

\displaystyle:=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},(\widetilde{h}_{1})^{\prime}_{pq},\cdots,(\widetilde{h}_{d})^{\prime}_{pq}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},(\widetilde{h}_{1})_{pq}\varepsilon_{pq}^{-1/d},\cdots,(\widetilde{h}_{d})_{pq}\varepsilon_{pq}^{-1/d}\right)~.

(285)

Note that

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{pq}\widetilde{s}_{qr}\widetilde{s}_{rp}

\displaystyle=\big(\zeta^{+}_{pqr},\zeta^{-}_{pqr},(\eta_{1})_{pqr}\varepsilon_{pqr}^{-1/d},\cdots,(\eta_{d})_{pqr}\varepsilon_{pqr}^{-1/d}\big)~.

(286)

It follows directly from (
283
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{pq}\widetilde{s}_{qr}\widetilde{s}_{rp}

\displaystyle\in\mr{C}~.

(287)

Therefore, the transition functions,

\displaystyle s_{pq}:=[\widetilde{s}_{pq}]:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\big(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}^{d}\big)/\mr{C}~,

(288)

satisfy the cocycle condition

\displaystyle s_{pq}s_{qr}s_{rp}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}}^{d}(4)}~.

(289)

Moreover, because of (
260
) the
\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
-bundle
P\to{\mathbb{X}}
with transition functions
t_{pq}
satisfies

\left(p_{1}\right)_{*}(P)\cong\mr{OFr}(T{\mathbb{X}})~.

(290)

Thus we have constructed a
\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
-structure on
{\mathbb{X}}
. For
d=1
, this is precisely the standard
\mr{Spin}^{c}(4)
-structure.

It follows from the above argument that the set of
\mr{Spin}^{d}_{\mr{C}}(4)
-structures on
{\mathbb{X}}
is a torsor for the subgroup of elements

(c(1),\dots,c(d))\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})^{d}~.

(291)

that satisfy:

\sum_{u=1}^{d}\iota_{u}\circ r_{n_{u}}(c(u))=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}})~.

(292)

{ex}

Fix a positive even integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and consider the finite group

\mr{C}_{n}=\left\{(\zeta,\zeta,\mu)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mr{U(1)}~|~\mu^{n/2}\zeta=1\right\}~.

(293)

Then
\mr{C}_{n}
is generated by

(-1,-1,g)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mr{U(1)}~,

(294)

where
g\in\mr{U(1)}
is any element of order
n
. Moreover, since
n
is even,

\mr{C}_{n}\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}~.

(295)

We denote the corresonding generalized spin
{}^{\mr}{c}
group by

\mr{Spin}^{1}_{\mr{C}_{n}}(4)=\big(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}\big)/\mr{C}_{n}~.

(296)

As in Example
C
, we introduce transition functions
t_{pq}^{\mr{SO(4)}}
for
\mr{OFr}({\mathbb{X}})
and choose local lifts
(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-}):U_{pq}\to\mr{Spin(4)}
which satisfy a commutative diagram of the form (
260
). Therefore, as before, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-valued cochain

\displaystyle\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{t}^{\pm}_{pqr}\widetilde{t}^{\pm}_{pqr}\widetilde{t}^{\pm}_{pqr}~,

(297)

is a Čech representative of the second Stiefel-Whitney class of
T{\mathbb{X}}
:

\displaystyle[\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}]:=w_{2}(T{\mathbb{X}})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})~.

(298)

Let us introduce a complex line bundle
{\cal L}
with a first Chern class
c_{1}({\cal L})
which is at the moment an arbitrary integral class, but will be further constrained by a cohomological condition later. Endowing
{\cal L}
with a Hermitian metric on its fibers, we can regard
{\cal L}
as
\mr{U}(1)
-bundle. Let
\{h_{pq}\}
denote transition functions on this bundle. As in (
264
), we choose local lifts
\widetilde{h}_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{U(1)}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{pq}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U(1)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{h}_{pq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{pq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}

(299)

commutes, where
P_{n}
is the
n^{th}
power map, which acts as
z\mapsto z^{n}
for
z\in\mr{U(1)}
. Note that the global existence of the lifts
\widetilde{h}_{pq}
is equivalent to the existence of a complex line bundle
{\cal W}
such that
{\cal W}^{\otimes n}={\cal L}
. Existence of
{\cal W}
is equivalent to
c_{1}({\cal L})
being
n
times an integral class
y\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})
(which is then the first Chern class of
{\cal W}
, i.e.,
y=c_{1}({\cal W})
).

The cochain

\displaystyle\eta_{pqr}:=\widetilde{h}_{pq}\widetilde{h}_{qr}\widetilde{h}_{rp}~,

(300)

represents the
\mathsf{\,mod\,}~n
reduction of
c_{1}({\cal L})
:

\displaystyle[\eta_{pqr}]:=r_{n}\big(c_{1}({\cal L})\big)~,

(301)

where
r_{n}
is reduction modulo
n
, and measures the obstruction to the existence of a line bundle
{\cal W}
.

We define transition functions

\widetilde{t}_{pq}:=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},(\widetilde{h})_{pq}\right)~.

(302)

With this definition,

\widetilde{t}_{pq}\widetilde{t}_{qr}\widetilde{t}_{rp}=\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{+},\zeta_{pqr}^{-},\eta_{pqr}\right)~.

(303)

It follows from the definition (
293
) that if

\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}\cdot\big(\eta_{pqr}\big)^{n/2}=1~,

(304)

the transition functions
\widetilde{t}_{pq}
satisfy

\widetilde{t}_{pq}\widetilde{t}_{qr}\widetilde{t}_{rp}\in\mr{C}_{n}~,

(305)

as desired.
If there exist functions

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{pq}:U_{pq}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}}~,

(306)

such that

\displaystyle\iota(\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr})\cdot\big(\eta_{pqr}\big)^{n/2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{pq}\varepsilon_{qr}\varepsilon_{rp}\equiv\varepsilon_{pqr}~,

(307)

this is equivalent to the following cohomological condition

\boxed{\iota\big(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})\big)+\frac{n}{2}r_{n}\big(c_{1}({\cal L})\big)=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}})~,}

(308)

which should be viewed as a constraint on
c_{1}({\cal L})
.

We now show that the condition (
307
) (or equivalently (
308
)) enables us to satisfy the cocycle condition for
\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}_{n}}^{1}(4)
. We simply define the modified transition functions
60
60

60

We choose
a
solution
y
to
y^{n/2}=\varepsilon_{pq}
, use it in (
309
)–(
311
). The conclusion (
312
) remains true independent of the choice of branch for the
(n/2)^{th}
root because by definition, the
(n/2)^{th}
power of
any
solution is the same. The reasoning is similar to what was described in footnote
59
for Example
C
, except in this example, we do not have the option to be undemocratic with only one
\mr{U(1)}
bundle at our disposal.

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}^{\prime}_{pq}:=\widetilde{h}_{pq}(\varepsilon_{pq})^{-2/n}~,

(309)

and maps
\widetilde{s}_{pq}:U_{pq}\to\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}
via

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{pq}

\displaystyle:=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},\widetilde{h}^{\prime}_{pq}\right)=\left(\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{+},\widetilde{t}_{pq}^{-},\widetilde{h}_{pq}\varepsilon_{pq}^{-2/n}\right)~.

(310)

Note that

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{pq}\widetilde{s}_{qr}\widetilde{s}_{rp}

\displaystyle=\big(\zeta^{+}_{pqr},\zeta^{-}_{pqr},\eta_{pqr}\varepsilon_{pqr}^{-2/n}\big)~.

(311)

It follows directly from (
307
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{pq}\widetilde{s}_{qr}\widetilde{s}_{rp}

\displaystyle\in\mr{C}_{n}~.

(312)

Therefore, the transition functions,

\displaystyle s_{pq}:=[\widetilde{s}_{pq}]:U_{pq}\longrightarrow\big(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{U(1)}\big)/\mr{C}_{n}~,

(313)

satisfy the cocycle condition

\displaystyle s_{pq}s_{qr}s_{rp}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\mr{Spin}_{\mr{C}_{n}}^{d}(4)}~.

(314)

A few remarks are in order. First of all, if
n=2
, the map
\iota
reduces to the identity map, and the condition (
308
) reduces to the condition for a standard spin
{}^{\mr{c}}
structure, namely
w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=c_{1}({\cal L})\mathsf{\,mod\,}~2
. In this case, one recovers the familiar fact that the evenness of
c_{1}({\cal L})
is equivalent to the manifold being a spin manifold.

For
n>2
, if
c_{1}({\cal L})
is
n
times an integral class, i.e., if there exists an integral class
y\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
c_{1}({\cal L})=n\cdot y
, then the cohomological condition (
308
) is equivalent to
\iota(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}}))=0
. In this case, one possibility is that
{\mathbb{X}}
be spin, i.e.,
w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=0
. The other possibility is that
w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})
be in the kernel of the map
\iota:H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}})
. This possibility merits further study.

Appendix D
Superconformal Multiplets

In this section, we will briefly review superconformal multiplets for the
d=4

{\cal N}=2
superconformal algebra
\mf{su}(2,2|2)
.
61
61

61

This is the real form appropriate to
d=4
Lorentzian signature, which is implicit in standard treatments of radial quantization. The appropriate real form for
d=4
Euclidean signature is
\mathfrak{su^{*}(4|2)}
, which would lead to a noncompact summand in the Euclidean
\mr{R}
-symmetry group. This distinction will be unimportant for us, however, see
CMRS (23)
for a discussion in the context of topological twisting.

The reader is referred to
CDI16b ()
;
RR (14)
;
DO (02)
;
Ebe (20)
for more details.
The superconformal algebra admits a bosonic subalgebra,

\displaystyle\mf{su}(2,2)\oplus\mf{su}(2)_{\mr{R}}\oplus\mf{u(1)}_{\mr{R}}\subset\mf{su}(2,2|2)~.

(315)

Note that
\mf{su}(2,2)\cong\mf{so}(4,2)
, and there is an
\mf{su}(2)_{\mr{R}}\oplus\mf{u}(1)_{\mr{R}}
symmetry, we will denote the charge under the
\mr{R}
-symmetry by
(R;r)
where
R\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
is the Dynkin label for the
\mf{su}(2)_{\mr{R}}
and
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
is the charge under
\mf{u}(1)_{\mr{R}}
. The scaling dimension will be denoted by
\Delta
and the Dynkin labels for the representation under the (spin lift of) the 4d rotation group
\mr{Spin(4)}\cong\mr{SU(2)}\times\mr{SU(2)}
will be denoted by
(j_{1};j_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. An operator with these eigenvalues under the generators of the superconformal algebra will then be denoted by
[j_{1};j_{2}]_{\Delta}^{(R;r)}
. In particular, the supercharges transform in the following representations:

{\cal Q}\in[1;0]_{\frac{1}{2}}^{(1;-1)},\quad\overline{{\cal Q}}\in[0;1]_{\frac{1}{2}}^{(1;1)}~.

(316)

The basic generating multiplets are the long multiplets and short multiplets satisfying certain unitarity bounds
Min (97)
. Short multiplets are obtained from the long multiplet by imposing certain shortening conditions. These multiplets are listed in Tables
D.1
and
D.2
.

{\cal Q}
and
\overline{{\cal Q}}
-shortening conditions which leads to restriction on some of the quantum numbers. The consistent two-sided multiplets are listed below.

for details on the restrictions on the quantum numbers.

Note that the scaling dimensions of the short multiplets are determined by shortening conditions; this is not indicated in the notation.

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{R,r(j_{1},j_{2})}^{\Delta}

\displaystyle=L\overline{L}[j_{1};j_{2}]_{\Delta}^{(R;r)}~,

(317)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{R,r(j_{1},j_{2})}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}\overline{L}[j_{1};j_{2}]^{(R;r)}~,\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{R,r(0,j_{2})}

\displaystyle=B_{1}\overline{L}[0;j_{2}]^{(R>0;r)}~,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{r(0,j_{2})}

\displaystyle=B_{1}\overline{L}[0;j_{2}]^{(0;r)}~,\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R(j_{1},j_{2})}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{R(0,j_{2})}

where
\ell,\overline{\ell}=1,2
. The conjugate multiplets
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{R,r(j_{1},j_{1})},\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{R,r(j_{1},0)},\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{r(j_{1},0)}
, and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{R(j_{1},0)}
can be obtained by complex conjugation. The full operator content of each of these multiplets is tabulated in
CDI16b ()
.

Vectormultiplets realize the short multiplet
A_{2}\overline{B}_{1}[0;0]^{(0;1)}
and hypermultiplets realize the short multiplet
B_{1}\overline{B}_{1}[0;0]^{(1;0)}
.
Superconformal multiplets containing currents for symmetries are called conserved current multiplets. Here we are only interested in the flavor current multiplet and the stress tensor multiplet (also called the energy-momentum multiplet). See
CDI16b ()
for other conserved current multiplets.

\displaystyle\left[{\cal Q}_{A\alpha},\mu^{(\!(BC)\!)a}\right]

\displaystyle=\delta_{A}{}^{(\!(B}\psi_{\alpha}^{C)\!)a}\in[1;0]_{\frac{5}{2}}^{(1;-1)}~,

The current

is the conserved current for the flavor symmetry of the theory.

Stress tensor multiplet.

The multiplet
A_{2}\overline{A}_{2}[0;0]_{2}^{(0;0)}
is the stress tensor multiplet whose top component is the stress tensor of the theory. The generators of the
d=4

{\cal N}=2
superconformal algebra must be integrals of local currents over a spatial slice. The bosonic generators are integrals of the stress tensor
T_{\mu\nu}
and the
\mf{su}(2)_{\mr{R}}\oplus\mf{u}(1)_{\mr{R}}

\mr{R}
-symmetry currents
(j_{\mu}^{(\mr{R})})^{A}
,
j_{\mu}^{(\mr{R})}
. The fermionic generators are integrals of conserved fermionic supercurrents
G_{\mu\alpha}^{A}
and
\widetilde{G}_{\mu\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}^{A}
. The superconformal algebra dictates that the stress tensor,
\mr{R}
-symmetry current and supercurrent sit in the same multiplet, called the stress tensor multiplet. In particular,

\{{\cal Q}_{\alpha A},\widetilde{G}^{B}_{\mu\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}\}=\sigma^{\nu}_{\alpha\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}\delta_{A}{}^{B}T_{\mu\nu}~,\quad\{\widetilde{{\cal Q}}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}A},G^{B}_{\mu\beta}\}=\widetilde{\sigma}^{\nu}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\beta}\delta_{A}{}^{B}T_{\mu\nu}~.

(322)

The full stress tensor multiplet has the form
CDI16b ()
;

shown in Fig.
D.2
.
