Appendix E
The Superconformal Index

In this appendix, we review the superconformal index. For recent reviews, see
RR (14)
;
RR (16)
;
Gad (20)
. First, let us review the supersymmetric index as a trace over the Hilbert space in the Hamiltonian quantization of a generic supersymmetric theory in
d
spacetime dimensions, coupled to conserved currents of some global symmetries (i.e., to background flavor bundles). To compute it, we pick a supercharge
65
65

65

In terms of background supergravity, this is a choice of generalized Killing spinor, i.e., a solution to the differential constraints obtained by demanding the vanishing of the supersymmetry variations of the gravitino, in a bosonic background where the auxiliary fields of the supergravity multiplet may have nontrivial profiles
FS (11)
;
KTZ (12)
;
CKM+ (12)
;
KZ (13)
;
KR (88)
;
CMRS (23)
.

{\cal Q}
with an adjoint
{\cal Q}^{+}
where
\{{\cal Q},{\cal Q}^{+}\}=2\mr{H}
, and
{\cal Q}^{2}=0
. In a unitary theory, this implies that the spectrum of
\mr{H}
is positive semidefinite. The index is simply a weighted supertrace of states in
{\mr{Ker}}\,\mr{H}
, i.e., states in the cohomology of
{\cal Q}
and
{\cal Q}^{+}
, or equivalently, the Hilbert space of a radially quantized theory on
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\times{\mathbb{R}}
. Concretely, the index has a trace formula of the form

\displaystyle{{\cal I}}(\{\mu_{i}\})

\displaystyle=\mr{Tr}_{{\mr{Ker}}\,\mr{H}}(-1)^{\mr{F}}e^{-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}T_{i}}e^{-2\beta\{{\cal Q},{\cal Q}^{+}\}}~,

(323)

where
\{T_{i}\}
is a complete set of generators that commute with
{\cal Q}
and with each other,
\{\mu_{i}\}
is a set of ‘‘fugacities,’’ or ‘‘chemical potentials’’ for the associated global symmetries
66
66

66

At the moment, we are including superconformal fugacities (finitely many in number) as well as fugacities associated with flavor symmetries (which could be arbitrarily many in number, and which commute with all generators of the superconformal algebra). We will shortly refine this definition.

and
\mr{F}
is the fermion number. As is the case for the original Witten index
Wit (82)
, only the states with
\mr{H}=0
contribute to the index, and consequently the index does not depend on
\beta
. The index so defined is a function of the fugacities that couple to the conserved charges of the corresponding global symmetries.
67
67

67

Sometimes, the factor
e^{-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}T_{i}}
is replaced by
\prod_{i}\widetilde{\mu}_{i}^{C_{i}}
with
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
referred to as the fugacities and
C_{i}
the conserved charges.

There are various refinements of the index, and specializations that are useful in different contexts, especially for probing enhanced supersymmetry
GRRY11b ()
.

Expressed as a trace over the full Hilbert space
{\cal H}
of states on
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
the
d=4

{\cal N}=2
superconformal index has the generic form

\displaystyle{{\cal I}}(p,q,t;\{a_{i}\})

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Tr}_{{\cal H}}(-1)^{\mr{F}}p^{\frac{1}{3}(\Delta+\frac{1}{2}j_{1}-R-r)+\frac{1}{2}j_{2}}q^{\frac{1}{3}(\Delta+\frac{1}{2}j_{1}-R-r)-\frac{1}{2}j_{2}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(R+r)}e^{-\beta(\Delta-j_{1}-R+\frac{1}{2}r)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mr{rk\,}G^{\mr{f}}}a_{i}^{f_{i}}~.

(324)

Recall that the index is written with respect to a choice of supercharge
68
68

68

The supercharges of the
d=4

{\cal N}=2
superconformal algebra in radial quantization are
{\cal Q}_{A\alpha},\widetilde{Q}^{A}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}},{\cal S}^{A\alpha}:={\cal Q}^{\dagger A\alpha},\widetilde{S}_{A}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}:=\widetilde{Q}_{A}^{\dagger\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}
. Here
A
is an
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
symmetry index, and
\alpha
and
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}
are spinor indices, which take values
+,-
and
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{+},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{-}
respectively. The supercharge used to define the index is
{\cal Q}:={\cal Q}_{1-}
, for which
\{{\cal Q}_{1-},({\cal Q}_{1-})^{\dagger}\}=\Delta-j_{1}-2R+\frac{1}{2}r
. This is the coefficient of
-\beta
in (
324
). This
{\cal Q}
should not be confused with the scalar supercharge in the bulk of the paper.

{\cal Q}
, which determines an
{\cal N}=1
subalgebra of an
{\cal N}=2
algebra. The commutant of
{\cal Q}
in the superconformal algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2,2|2)
is
\mathfrak{su}(2,1|1)
, which has rank
3
. Here
(p,q,t)
are the three superconformal fugacities, and
\{a_{i}\}
is a set of fugacities associated with global symmetries
G^{\mr{f}}
that commute with each other and with the superconformal algebra (and
\{f_{i}\}
are the conserved flavor charges),
\Delta
is the dilatation generator (a.k.a. the conformal Hamiltonian in radial quantization),
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
are the Cartan generators of the
\mr{SU(2)}_{1}\times\mr{SU(2)}_{2}
isometry group of the spatial
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
, and
R
and
r
are the Cartan generators of
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
and
\mr{U(1)_{R}}
respectively. As previously stated, the trace formula makes a choice of supercharge. The formula (
324
) is written for a supercharge with quantum numbers
\Delta=\frac{1}{2}R=-r=\frac{1}{2}
,
(j_{1},j_{2})=(0,-1)
.
69
69

69

As in the main text, we use the same symbols for the generators and their eigenvalues. The eigenvalues are normalized in accordance with Appendix
D
.

The superconformal index counts short multiplets modulo an equivalence relation that sets to zero the contributions of short multiplets that can recombine to long ones
KMMR (05)
.
70
70

70

A consequence of the definition (
324
) is that the index is zero on long multiplets, and therefore, it
cannot
be used to distinguish between two short multiplets for which it evaluates to the same result. See
GPR (09)
for a discussion of this issue.

Superconformal multiplets and their shortening conditions are briefly reviewed in Appendix
D
. (See
(
BLL+, 13
, App. B)
for further details).

The background
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
has been studied extensively in the context of supersymmetric localization (see
P+ (16)
for a review).
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
is a non-Kähler complex manifold that admits a Hermitian metric, and admits nontrivial holomorphic line bundles classified by a rank one Dolbeault cohomology group
{\cal H}^{0,1}({\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1})
(although complex line bundles on it are trivial since
H^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1},{\mathbb{Z}})=0
). The parameter dependence of the partition function on background fields is discussed in
CDFK (13)
;
CDFK (14)
, where it is argued that the path integral of a supersymmetric theory on a Hermitian manifold depends only on the complex structure deformations of the manifold.

While it is often remarked that the supersymmetric index is the partition function on
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, a more precise statement is that it is proportional to the partition function up to a scheme-independent (and hence physical) multiplicative factor involving the supersymmetric Casimir energy
ACM (14)
;
LM (14)
;
ACDP+ (15)
.
This multiplicative factor is dependent on the central charges
a_{4d}
and
c_{4d}
and reduces to
1
for a conformal theory.

In general, the three-parameter superconformal index (
324
) is not known in closed analytic form as a function of
(p,q,t)
. There is at least one technique for determining it in terms of a series expansion in these variables by exploiting generalized S-duality and recasting the problem to one of diagonalizing a set of commuting difference operators known as the elliptic Ruijsenaars-Schneider Hamiltonians
GRR (12)
;
Raz (13)
. The eigenfunctions of these operators are functions of
(p,q,t)
and one can reconstruct the index as a sum of products of these “wavefunctions,” weighted with coefficient functions that encode data at the punctures in Class
{\cal S}
theory. In principle, this algorithm can be used to determine a series expansion of
{{\cal I}}(p,q,t,\{a_{i}\})
in all fugacities.
71
71

71

See also
PP (21)
for some recent work on expressing the Schur index (with flavor fugacities) in closed form.

In practice, however, it is preferable to have a specialization that is computable with less effort and nevertheless contains useful information. One such specialization is the so-called Schur limit of the superconformal index, with the resulting index known as the Schur index.
72
72

72

Its name stems from the existence of closed-form expressions for the “wavefunctions,” in terms of Schur polynomials.

It can be obtained from (
324
) by setting
q=t
and noting that the
p
-dependence is
{\cal Q}
-exact and drops out
73
73

73

One uses the fact that the index is independent of
\beta
, and in particular, counts states with

\Delta-j_{1}-R+\frac{1}{2}r=0~.

This determines
j_{1}
for a given
(\Delta,r,R)
. The exponents of
p
,
q
, and
t
in (
324
) are combinations of Cartan generators that commute with the supercharge
{\cal Q}
used to define the index in the first place. The power of
p
in (
324
) becomes
\frac{1}{2}\big\{\widetilde{Q}_{1,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{+}},\big(\widetilde{Q}_{1,\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{+}}\big)^{\dagger}\big\}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta+j_{2}-R-\frac{1}{2}r)
– see footnote
68
. The
p
-dependence is therefore
{\cal Q}
-exact, and the index counts states with
\Delta+j_{2}-R-\frac{1}{2}r=0
. This simplification leads to (
325
)

leading to

\displaystyle{{\cal I}}_{\mr{Schur}}(q,\{a_{i}\})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Tr\,}_{{\cal H}({\mathbb{S}}^{3})}(-1)^{\mr{F}}q^{\Delta-\frac{R}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mr{rk}\,G^{\mr{f}}}a_{i}^{f_{i}}~.

(325)

The states counted by this index – known as Schur operators (or equivalently, Schur states) – are harmonic representatives of cohomology classes of a nilpotent supercharge defined by the sum of a Poincaré supercharge
{\cal Q}
and a conformal supercharge
S
.
74
74

74

The Schur index is an intrinsically
{\cal N}=2
index.

The Schur index is, in fact, related to
q
-deformed 2d topological Yang-Mills theory
GPRR (09)
;
GRRY11a ()
. It is now known that Schur operators form the structure of a two-dimensional chiral algebra
BLL+ (13)
;
BPRvR (14)
;
LP (14)
, and the Schur index is the supercharacter of the vacuum module of this chiral algebra. The Schur index also has interesting modularity properties related to conformal anomalies
Raz (12)
. Finally, the Schur index is also conjecturally related to a wall-crossing invariant
CS (15)
defined in terms of the protected spin character of
GMN (10)
. So, despite its apparent simplicity relative to the ‘‘full’’ superconformal index, it encodes a lot of useful information about the protected operator spectrum of the theory.
75
75

75

See also
GV (24)
for a relation between pure
\mr{SU(2)}
SYM and the SYK model, where Schur indices play an important role.

For a superconformal
{\cal N}=2
theory, the index
is
the partition function on
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
as stated above. A natural question is whether there is a supergravity background on which the partition function computes the superconformal index, with couplings to background
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity fields.
76
76

76

For the
{\cal N}=1
index, a background based on new minimal supergravity was found in
(
FS, 11
, Sec. 5)
.

Such superconformal backgrounds have been discussed in
HH (12)
;
Pes (14)
, with a detailed discussion in
PP (19)
, where explicit expressions may be found for the metric, the generalized Killing spinors, and profiles of background fields of
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity (in suitable parametrizations, bases of fields, and spinors).
77
77

77

It would be interesting to apply the methods of
CMRS (23)
to determine a background on which the partition function computes a “topologically twisted Schur index,” but as discussed in the main text, the interpretation of such an index would probably be appropriate only for a conformal theory. As is well known
Wit (88)
, the trace of the energy-momentum tensor for topologically twisted
{\cal N}=2
SYM is a total derivative: the theory has global scale invariance, but not conformal invariance.

(For a recent quick introduction to
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity, see
CMRS (23)
.) The background is an
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
fibration over
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, where the fiber
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
itself is a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
fibration over an interval. In this formalism, one can turn on flat connections for background
{\cal N}=2
vectormultiplets for a maximal torus of the flavor symmetry group. These correspond to the flavor fugacities appearing in the index.

To our knowledge, a mass deformation of the Schur index is not known. On the one hand, it is quite natural – from the standpoint of
{\cal N}=2
supergravity – to consider couplings to background
{\cal N}=2
vectormultiplets with their scalars tuned to mass deformations (e.g., as we did in (
116
)). However, for non-conformal theories, one cannot perform a Weyl rescaling from
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, and there is no state-operator correspondence. The interpretation of the resulting mass-deformed index is therefore less clear.
78
78

78

The authors of
CS (15)
propose an extension of the Schur index to non-conformal Lagrangian
{\cal N}=2
theories (with
G^{\mr{gauge}}
-vectormultiplets and hypermultiplets in some representation of
G^{\mr{gauge}}
) in terms of a matrix integral over the maximal torus of
G^{\mr{gauge}}
. However, they note that it is a priori unclear why this extension will lead to a protected observable, and that further investigation is merited. Another non-conformal generalization of the Schur index which is based on isolating a subalgebra of the superconformal algebra that includes only the isometries of
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
was proposed in
Dum (16)
, but remains an unpublished proposal at the time of writing this.

Appendix F
Lagrangian Analysis Of The
\mr{ffs}
Trinion Theory

In this appendix, we describe the
\mr{ffs}
trinion theory as a Lagrangian theory of a free hypermultiplet and present the action. Let
V
be the
N
-dimensional defining representation of
\mr{SU(N)}
. The field content of the theory is a hypermultiplet valued in the quaternionic space
E\oplus\overline{E}
where
E
is the representation
V\otimes\overline{V}
of
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
with
\mr{U(1)}
acting as scalars.
79
79

79

In fact we could have chosen global symmetry group
\mr{U(N)}\times\mr{U(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
but a subgroup of the center isomorphic to
\mr{U(1)}\times\mr{U(1)}
acts trivially, so we choose to take
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
for simplicity. Otherwise, we would need to discuss non-effective group actions and stacks. This is beyond the scope of this paper.

To write the Lagrangian, we write the hypermultiplet in terms of a pair of
\mathcal{N}=1
chiral multiplets. The field content is same as a chiral multiplet
Q^{m\overline{n}}
and an anti-chiral multiplet
\widetilde{Q}^{\prime m\overline{n}}
both in
V\otimes\overline{V}
.
80
80

80

An anti-chiral multiplet is distinguished from a chiral multiplet by having a Weyl spinor of the opposite chirality. In Euclidean signature, chiral and antichiral multiplets are independent and unrelated by complex conjugation: the complex conjugate of a chiral multiplet is another chiral multiplet.

Let us write their component fields as
81
81

81

Here
m,n=1,\ldots,N
are indices for
V
, and
\overline{m},\overline{n}=1,\ldots,N
are indices for
\overline{V}
. We use the convention that upper left (right) indices are for the first (second) factor and lower left (right) indices are for the dual space of the first (second) factor. We could equivalently have treated
(q^{2})^{m\overline{n}}
and
(\widetilde{q}^{1})^{\overline{n}m}
as independent scalars.

Q^{m\overline{n}}=\left((q^{2})^{m\overline{n}},(\psi_{\alpha})^{m\overline{n}}\right)~,\quad\widetilde{Q}^{\prime m\overline{n}}=\left((q^{1})^{m\overline{n}},(\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}})^{m\overline{n}}\right)~.

(326)

Define complex scalars
\widetilde{q}^{A,\overline{n}m}
valued in
\overline{V}\otimes V
via

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}^{A,\overline{n}m}=(q_{A,m\overline{n}})^{\dagger}=\Omega^{m\overline{n},\overline{n}^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{AB}q_{B,m^{\prime}\overline{n}^{\prime}}~,

(327)

where
\Omega
is a symplectic form
82
82

82

There exists a choice of basis exhibiting
V\otimes\overline{V}\oplus\overline{V}\otimes V
as a real vector space with real dimension
4N^{2}
. In this basis,
\Omega
is a
2N^{2}\times 2N^{2}
matrix with a block diagonal form, with
N^{2}
blocks each of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

on
V\otimes\overline{V}\oplus\overline{V}\otimes V
, and
\varepsilon^{AB}
is the completely antisymmetric invariant tensor of
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{\mr{R}}
, with
\varepsilon^{12}=1
. The matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}q^{1}&q^{2}\\
-\widetilde{q}_{1}&\widetilde{q}_{2}\end{array}\right)~,

(328)

(with
V\otimes\overline{V}
indices suppressed) is then a quaternion in the standard quaternionic structure on
V\otimes\overline{V}\oplus\overline{V}\otimes V
and
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
acts on the scalars by right multiplication by unit quaternions.

For later use, we also define conjugate spinors

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi^{\alpha,\overline{n}m}&:=\big(\psi_{\alpha,m\overline{n}}\big)^{\dagger}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\Omega^{m\overline{n},\overline{n}^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\psi_{\beta,m^{\prime}\overline{n}^{\prime}}~,\\
\widetilde{\psi}^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha},\overline{n}m}&:=\big(\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha},m\overline{n}}\big)^{\dagger}=\varepsilon^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}\Omega^{m\overline{n},\overline{n}^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta},m^{\prime}\overline{n}^{\prime}}~.\end{split}

(329)

Let
\widetilde{P}_{T}
denote a principal
\widetilde{G}_{T}
bundle over
{\mathbb{X}}
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{T}:=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}~.

(330)

For this bundle to exist,
{\mathbb{X}}
must be a spin manifold. We assume this is so for the moment. In the main text, we have discussed how this can be relaxed by defining the theory in terms of a
\widetilde{G}_{T}/C_{T}
bundle, for a suitable choice of subgroup
C_{T}
of the center of
\widetilde{G}_{T}
.

To write the action in curved space, we follow the procedure outlined in section
7
or
KR (88)
;
CMRS (23)
, coupling the hypermultiplet theory to a
bosonic
background of off-shell
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity that preserves
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry. Such a background can be defined by consistently setting the gravitinos and auxiliary spinor fields in the
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity multiplet to zero, as well as their supersymmetry variations to zero.
83
83

83

For the untwisted theory, this leads to a differential constraint on the supersymmetry parameter. However, as is well known from
Wit (88)
;
KR (88)
;
CMRS (23)
, upon twisting, there is a globally-defined scalar supersymmetry parameter corresponding to
{\cal Q}
symmetry, and no differential constraints are needed. The bosonic auxiliary fields of the supergravity multiplet are allowed to have nontrivial profiles consistent with supersymmetry
FS (11)
. However, for simplicity we assume that the 2-form field in the
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity multiplet is set to zero:
T_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}=0
, as this simplifies our partition function. For examples of more general backgrounds, see
HH (12)
;
Pes (14)
.

The supergravity background contains a connection for
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\mr{U(1)_{R}}
symmetry, but we treat the
\mr{U(1)_{R}}
symmetry as rigid and gauge its connection to zero (see
CMRS (23)
for details). The
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
connection plays an important role in the specialization of twisting, discussed later. We also couple to background vectormultiplets for the aforementioned
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
flavor symmetry: to this end, we introduce connections
A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU(N)}^{(1)}}
,
A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU(N)}^{(2)}}
,
A_{\mu}^{\mr{U(1)}}
. Although we write separate connections to clarify how they contribute to the covariant derivatives, it is useful to think of a composite background vectormultiplet for the
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
flavor symmetry. We denote the composite curvature for this background vectormultiplet by
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
where
a,b=1,\ldots,2(\mr{N}^{2}-1)+1
run over the generators of the Lie algebra of the gauge group as usual. The background vectormultiplet also contains an adjoint-valued auxiliary field
D_{AB}^{a}
which transforms as a triplet of
\mathsf{SU(2)_{R}}
.

The action of the free hypermultiplet coupled to background fields is

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{\mr{Hyper}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)\bigg[\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{f}}_{\mu}(\widetilde{q}_{A})^{\overline{n}m}\bm{\nabla}_{\mr{f}}^{\mu}(q^{A})_{m\overline{n}}-\frac{1}{24}\big(\mathscr{R}+3\mathscr{D}\big)(\widetilde{q}_{A})^{\overline{n}m}(q^{A})_{m\overline{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\quad+(\widetilde{q}_{A})^{\overline{n}m}D^{AB}(q_{B})_{m\overline{n}}+\mathsf{i}\widetilde{\psi}^{\overline{n}m}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\psi_{m\overline{n}}-\mathsf{i}\chi^{\overline{n}m}\not{D}^{-}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}\widetilde{\chi}_{m\overline{n}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)\left[\mr{Im\,}\tau_{ab}\left(\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{\mu\nu,b}-\frac{1}{2}D_{AB}^{a}D^{AB,b}\right)+\frac{\mathsf{i}}{32\pi}\tau_{ab}(F^{a}\wedge F^{b})\right]~.

(331)

Here,
\mathscr{D}
is the auxiliary scalar in the
{\cal N}=2
conformal supergravity multiplet,
\mathscr{R}
is the Ricci scalar curvature of
{\mathbb{X}}
,
\bm{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\mr{f}}
(or
\bm{\nabla}^{\mu}_{\mr{f}}
) are (metric + background gauge (flavor
\mr{f}
) +
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
)-covariant derivatives, and
\not{D}^{+}
denotes the chiral Dirac operator coupled to background gauge (flavor) and
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
connections – we will momentarily write their explicit expressions.

We have made the following important specialization of the background vectormultiplet: we have set its scalars to zero (to preserve the full flavor symmetry) and we have set its fermions to zero as doing so eliminates coupling terms between spinors in the hypermultiplet and the spinors in the background vectormultiplet. However, this imposes an algebraic constraint on
D_{AB}^{a}
in terms of the supersymmetry parameter defining the
{\cal N}=2
supergravity background and the curvature
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
.
84
84

84

Upon further specialization to the twisted theory, this constraint will simplify to (
342
). The supersymmetry constraints on the background vectormultiplet can be easily deduced from the results of
CMRS (23)
, but are not needed to follow the present discussion.

The covariant derivatives appearing above are connections on associated bundles induced from the
\widetilde{G}_{T}
-connection
\bm{\nabla}_{T}
on
\widetilde{P}_{T}
, and
\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}
denotes the Dirac operator coupled to the vector bundle
{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}
associated to
\widetilde{P}_{T}
by the representation
V\otimes\overline{V}
of
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
.

Let
\omega_{\mu}
,
A_{\mu}^{\mr{R}}
,
A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(1)}}
,
A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(2)}}
and
A_{\mu}^{\mr{U(1)}}
denote local coordinate representatives of the connections on vector bundles associated to
\left(p_{1}\right)_{*}\left(\bm{\nabla}_{T}\right),\left(p_{2}\right)_{*}\left(\bm{\nabla}_{T}\right),\left(p_{3}\right)_{*}\left(\bm{\nabla}_{T}\right)
,

\left(p_{4}\right)_{*}\left(\bm{\nabla}_{T}\right)
, and
\left(p_{5}\right)_{*}\left(\bm{\nabla}_{T}\right)
respectively. Here
\omega_{\mu}
is the spin connection. The covariant derivatives are

\displaystyle\hskip-10.84006pt\bm{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\mr{f}}q^{A}{}_{m\overline{n}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}q^{A}{}_{m\overline{n}}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{R}}\big)^{A}{}_{B}~q^{B}{}_{m\overline{n}}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(1)}}\big)^{m^{\prime}}{}_{m}~q^{2}{}_{m^{\prime}\overline{n}}\delta^{A}_{2}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(2)}}\big)^{\overline{n}^{\prime}}{}_{\overline{n}}~q^{1}{}_{m\overline{n}^{\prime}}\delta^{A}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{\mu}^{\mr{U(1)}}q^{A}{}_{m\overline{n}}~,

(332)

\displaystyle\hskip-10.84006pt\bm{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\mr{f}}\widetilde{q}_{A}{}^{\overline{m}\,n}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{q}_{A}{}^{\overline{m}\,n}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{R}}\big)^{B}{}_{A}~\widetilde{q}_{B}{}^{\overline{m}\,n}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(1)}}\big)^{\overline{m}}{}_{\overline{m}^{\prime}}~\widetilde{q}_{2}{}^{\overline{m}^{\prime}\,n}\delta_{A}^{2}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(2)}}\big)^{n}{}_{n^{\prime}}~\widetilde{q}_{1}{}^{\overline{m}\,n^{\prime}}\delta_{A}^{1}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{\mu}^{\mr{U(1)}}\widetilde{q}_{A}{}^{\overline{m}\,n}~,

(333)

\displaystyle\hskip-10.84006pt(D_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}})_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha m\overline{n}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha m\overline{n}}-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\mu}{}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}}(\sigma_{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}})^{\beta}{}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta m\overline{n}}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU(N)}^{(1)}}\big)^{m^{\prime}}{}_{m}\psi_{\alpha m^{\prime}\overline{n}}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(2)}}\big)^{\overline{n}^{\prime}}{}_{\overline{n}}\psi_{\alpha m\overline{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{\mu}^{\mr{U(1)}}\psi_{\alpha m\overline{n}}~,

(334)

\displaystyle\hskip-10.84006pt(D_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}})_{\mu}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}{}^{m\,\overline{n}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}{}^{m\,\overline{n}}-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\mu}{}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}})^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}{}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}{}^{m\,\overline{n}}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU(N)}^{(1)}}\big)^{m}{}_{m^{\prime}}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}{}^{m^{\prime}\,\overline{n}}+\big(A_{\mu}^{\mr{SU}(N)^{(2)}}\big)^{\overline{n}}{}_{\overline{n}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}{}^{m\,\overline{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{\mu}^{\mr{U(1)}}\widetilde{\chi}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}{}^{m\,\overline{n}}~,

(335)

Recall that for chiral spinors valued in a vector bundle
E\to{\mathbb{X}}
with connection, the chiral Dirac operators are maps

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{D}^{+}_{E}:\Gamma(S^{+}\otimes E)&\to\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes E)~,\\
\not{D}^{-}_{E}:\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes E)&\to\Gamma(S^{+}\otimes E)~,\end{split}

(336)

where
S^{\pm}
are chiral spinor bundles on
{\mathbb{X}}
.
85
85

85

For a complex vector bundle
E
, the adjoint of
D^{+}_{E}
is
(\not{D}^{+}_{E})^{\dagger}=\not{D}^{-}_{E}
.

The corresponding Dirac operator is

\displaystyle\not{D}_{E}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathsf{i}\not{D}^{-}_{E}\\
\mathsf{i}\not{D}^{+}_{E}&0\end{pmatrix}~,

(337)

in a basis determined by the chiral splitting. For us,

\not{D}^{+}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}:=\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mu}\big(D_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}\big)_{\mu}~,\quad\not{D}^{-}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}:=\sigma^{\mu}\big(D_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}\big)_{\mu}~.

(338)

The partition function of the theory is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}

\displaystyle=\int[{\cal D}\widetilde{q}{\cal D}q{\cal D}\widetilde{\psi}{\cal D}\psi{\cal D}\widetilde{\chi}{\cal D}\chi]e^{-{\cal S}_{\mr{Hyper}}}

\displaystyle=\mr{Det}\big(\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\big)\mr{Det}\big(-\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\big)\int[{\cal D}\widetilde{q}{\cal D}q]e^{-S_{\mr{Hyper}}^{\mr{Bosonic}}}~,

(339)

where
S_{\mr{Hyper}}^{\mr{Bosonic}}
is the bosonic part of the action (
331
), and
\mr{Det}\big(\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\big)\mr{Det}\big(-\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\big)
is a section of the determinant line bundle

\mr{DET}\big(\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\big)\mr{DET}\big(-\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\big)~.

(340)

Recall that the determinant line bundle of a chiral Dirac operator coupled to a bundle
E
with connection has fiber

\left(\mr{\Lambda}^{\mr{max}}{\mr{Ker}}(\not{D}^{\pm}_{E})\right)^{\vee}\otimes\mr{\Lambda}^{\mr{max}}{\mr{Cok}}(\not{D}^{\pm}_{E})\cong\left(\mr{\Lambda}^{\mr{max}}{\mr{Ker}}(\not{D}^{\pm}_{E})\right)^{\vee}\otimes\mr{\Lambda}^{\mr{max}}{\mr{Ker}}(\not{D}^{\pm}_{E})^{\dagger}~,

(341)

over the space of connections on
\widetilde{P}_{T}
.
Note that
\mr{DET}\big(-\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\big)
is dual to
\mr{DET}\big(\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\big)
, therefore (
340
) is isomorphic to the trivial line bundle, and therefore, the partition function of a free hypermultiplet is just a
c
-number, as expected.

F.1
Topological Twisting Of The
\mr{ffs}
Theory

Upon twisting,
q_{A}
becomes
M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}
and
\widetilde{q}_{A}
becomes
\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}
. Note that
M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\in\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}})
and
\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\in\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}\otimes{\cal V})
.
After topological twisting, for
{\cal Q}
-symmetry, and in particular, for
{\cal S}_{\mr{Hyper}}
to define a
{\cal Q}
-exact energy-momentum tensor – as we have already noted in section
7
– the fermions in the background vectormultiplet must be set to zero, and therefore, their
{\cal Q}
-variations must be required to vanish for consistency. In particular, the vanishing of the
{\cal Q}
-variation of
\chi_{\mu\nu}
yields the condition

\displaystyle D_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=F_{\mu\nu}^{+}~,\text{ or equivalently }D_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}=F_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}~,

(342)

where
F_{\mu\nu}
is the composite curvature for the background
\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}
connection (which, for purposes of writing the covariant derivatives above, was split into three connections for the three individual factors) and
D_{\mu\nu}
is the self-dual 2-form auxiliary field of the background vectormultiplet. We also set

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\mathscr{R}~,

(343)

to specialize to the twisted background.
86
86

86

See
KR (88)
;
CMRS (23)
for a derivation of this condition.

To obtain the twisted action
S^{\mr{twisted}}_{\mr{hyp}}
from the untwisted action (
331
) we use the constraints (
342
) and (
343
) and the Lichnerowicz identity
Lic (63)
87
87

87

In standard mathematical literature, and indeed if the conventions/normalizations of
CMRS (23)
were used, the Lichnerowicz identity would have the more familiar form

\displaystyle\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\mr{f}}\bm{\nabla}_{\mr{f}}^{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{4}\mathscr{R}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}F^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}~.

However, the conventions of this paper differ slightly from
CMRS (23)
, leading to (
344
).

\displaystyle\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\mr{f}}\bm{\nabla}_{\mr{f}}^{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{16}\mathscr{R}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}+F^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}~.

(344)

to combine terms in the twisted action containing
M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}
and
\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{f}}_{\mu}\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\bm{\nabla}_{\mr{f}}^{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{16}\mathscr{R}\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}+\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}F^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{by parts}}{\overset{\text{integrate}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)\left(\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}\bm{\nabla}^{\mr{f}}_{\mu}\bm{\nabla}_{\mr{f}}^{\mu}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{16}\mathscr{R}\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}M^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}+\widetilde{M}_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}}F^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\alpha}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}M_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\beta}}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:lichnerowicz}}}{\overset{\text{Lichnerowicz}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\mr{vol}(g)\widetilde{M}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}M~.

(345)

The path integral of the twisted theory is expressed as

\mr{Z}_{T}^{\mr{twisted}}=\exp\left(-\frac{\mr{i}}{16\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\tau_{ab}~(F^{a}\wedge F^{b})\right)\int[{\cal D}\mr{HM}]e^{-S^{\mr{twisted}}_{\mr{hyp}}}~,

(346)

where
S^{\mr{twisted}}_{\mr{hyp}}
is the twisted version of the hypermulitplet part of the action of equation (
120
).
Thus the Gaussian path integral in (
346
) yields

\begin{split}\int[{\cal D}\mr{HM}]e^{-S^{\mr{twisted}}_{\mr{hyp}}}=\left(\mr{Det}\left(\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\right)\right)^{-1}\mr{Det}\big(\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+}\big)\mr{Det}\big(-\mathsf{i}\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-}\big)=1~.\end{split}

(347)

Because of
{\cal Q}
-symmetry, the determinants cancel. The prefactor in (
346
) is a topological invariant, and in fact,

\mr{Z}_{T}^{\mr{twisted}}=\exp\left(-\frac{\mr{i}}{16\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{X}}}\tau_{ab}~(F^{a}\wedge F^{b})\right)~.

(348)

F.2
Central Action And Extension Of Domain

Since the two determinant line bundles in (
340
) are dual, the action of the global symmetry associated to the center of
\widetilde{G}_{T}
acts trivially on the product. Therefore we cannot use that expression to derive
C_{T}^{\mr{max}}
. However, a further extension of the domain
allows us to derive this group.
Note that equation (
331
) just involves massless free bosons and fermions coupled to external fields. For trivial background fields, the flavor symmetry of the action is in fact larger than the product of flavor
symmetries associated to the separate punctures (as often happens in theories of class
{\cal S}
). Let

G_{f}:=\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{SU(N)}\times\mr{U(1)}~.

(349)

There is (classically) a
G_{f}^{+}\times G_{f}^{-}
symmetry where
G_{f}^{+}
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\chi}
,
\chi
and acts on
\widetilde{\psi}
,
\psi
in the standard way. Similarly,
G_{f}^{-}
acts trivially on

\widetilde{\psi}
,
\psi
. We introduce a background bundle with connection for
G_{f}^{+}\times G_{f}^{-}
. The coupling to the bosonic fields breaks
\mr{SU(2)_{R}}
symmetry by choosing a complex structure for the quaternionic target space: We couple

G_{f}^{+}
to the chiral multiplets
Q^{m\overline{n}}
and
(\widetilde{Q}^{\prime m\overline{n}})^{\dagger}
, and
G_{f}^{-}
to the anti-chiral multiplets
(\widetilde{Q}^{m\overline{n}})^{\dagger}
and
\widetilde{Q}^{\prime m\overline{n}}
.
Then our full symmetry group is extended to:

\widetilde{G}_{T}^{\mr{extended}}=\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times G_{f}^{+}\times G_{f}^{-}~,

(350)

Now we can consider the chiral actions of subgroups of the center. Elements of
C_{T}^{+}

are of the form

(\zeta,1,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\xi^{+},1_{G_{f}^{-}})~,

(351)

while elements of
C_{T}^{-}
are of the form

(1,\zeta,\zeta,1_{G_{f}^{+}},\omega^{k_{1}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\xi^{-})~,

(352)

The action on the partition function is:

\hskip-7.22743pt(\zeta,1,\zeta,\omega^{k_{1}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\xi^{+},1_{G_{f}^{-}})\cdot\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}(\widetilde{P}_{T},\widetilde{\bm{\nabla}}_{T})=(\zeta\omega^{k_{1}^{+}}\overline{\omega}^{k_{2}^{+}}\xi^{+})^{\mr{Ind}(\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{+})}\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}(\widetilde{P}_{T},\widetilde{\bm{\nabla}}_{T})~.

(353)

\hskip-10.84006pt(1,\zeta,\zeta,1_{G_{f}^{+}},\omega^{k_{1}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\omega^{k_{2}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{N},\xi^{-},)\cdot\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}(\widetilde{P}_{T},\widetilde{\bm{\nabla}}_{T})=(\zeta\omega^{k_{1}^{-}}\overline{\omega}^{k_{2}^{-}}\xi^{-})^{\mr{Ind}(\not{D}_{{\cal V}\otimes\overline{{\cal V}}}^{-})}\widetilde{\mr{Z}}_{T}(\widetilde{P}_{T},\widetilde{\bm{\nabla}}_{T})~.

(354)

Demanding that these actions are trivial implies:

\xi^{\pm}=\zeta\omega^{-k_{1}^{\pm}+k_{2}^{\pm}}~,

(355)

and we thus produce invariance groups
C_{T}^{\pm,\mr{max}}
isomorphic to
C_{T}^{\mr{max}}
in (
165
). We can, of course, restrict to the
vectorlike couplings used above.
In general, we expect it is important to use the maximal symmetry group to find the most stringent conditions for defining
C_{T}^{\mr{max}}
.

Appendix G
Examples Of Cohomological Conditions For
A_{N-1}
Class
{\cal S}
Theories With Full And Simple Punctures

In this section, we will derive cohomological conditions for the existence of weakly coupled descriptions of
A_{N-1}
class
{\cal S}
theories on general non-spin manifolds following the discussion in section
8
. These cohomological conditions depend on the chosen pants decomposition for the weakly coupled description of the theory. For simplicity, we will restrict to the theories constructed from trinions with
\mr{ffs}
and
\mr{fff}
punctures and only full punctures being gauged.

Let us briefly recall the notation from section
8
. Choose a pants decomposition of
C_{g,n}
into a set
{\cal T}
of trinions. The external cutting curves are denoted by
c_{i}
and internal cutting curves are denoted by
c_{\alpha}
. We will denote the external cutting curve bounding a full (resp. simple) puncture by
c_{i}^{\mr{f}}
(resp.
c_{i}^{\mr{s}}
).

For the cutting curves bounding a trinion
T
, we will write
c_{T,i}
,
c_{T,\alpha}
. The simply connected cover of the flavor group at an external cutting curve
c_{i}
is denoted by
\widetilde{G}_{c_{i}}
and the simply connected cover of the gauge group at an internal cutting curve
c_{\alpha}
is denoted by
\mr{SU(N)}_{c_{\alpha}}
. Define the group

G_{\mathsf{S}}=\bigg(\mr{Spin(4)}\times\mr{SU(2)_{R}}\times\prod_{i}\widetilde{G}_{i}\times\prod_{\alpha}\mr{SU(N)}_{\alpha}\bigg)\bigg/C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}}~,

(356)

where
C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}}
is defined below (
195
).

The cohomological conditions for the theory are obtained from the existence of the principal
G_{\mr{S}}
bundle
P_{\mr{S}}\to{\mathbb{X}}
.
To determine these cohomological conditions, we will follow the same strategy as in section
4
. Let
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3,c_{i}},p_{4,c_{\alpha}}
be projection maps to various factors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{\mathsf{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Spin(4)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{SU(2)_{R}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}_{c_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{SU(N)}_{c_{\alpha}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3,c_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{4,c_{\alpha}}}

(357)

Let us also denote the transfer of structure groups of
P_{\mr{S}}
along various projections by

\begin{split}\mr{Fr}({\mathbb{X}})&:=(p_{1})_{*}(P_{\mr{S}})~,\\
P^{\mr{R}}&:=(p_{2})_{*}(P_{\mr{S}})~,\\
P^{\mr{f},c_{i}}&:=(p_{3,c_{i}})_{*}(P_{\mr{S}})~,\\
P^{\mr{gauge},c_{\alpha}}&:=(p_{4,c_{\alpha}})_{*}(P_{\mr{S}})~.\end{split}

(358)

Define a set of positive integers
n_{c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}
by

p_{3,c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}(C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/n_{c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}{\mathbb{Z}}~.

(359)

Let us write

C_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}}:=p_{3,c_{i}^{\mr{f}}}(C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}})\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}~,\quad C_{c_{\alpha}}:=p_{4,c_{\alpha}}(C^{\prime\prime}_{{\cal T}})\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}~.

(360)

We can then introduce flat
C_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}}
,
C_{c_{\alpha}}
-gerbes
b_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}}
,
b_{c_{\alpha}}
respectively. Their characteristic classes

\mu(b_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},C_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}})~,\quad\mu(b_{c_{\alpha}})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},C_{c_{\alpha}})~,

(361)

can be identified with the obstruction to lifting the structure groups of
P^{\mr{f},c_{i}^{\mr{f}}}
and
P^{\mr{gauge},c_{\alpha}}
respectively from
\mr{SU(N)}/C^{\mr{flavor}}_{i,\mr{f}}
and
\mr{SU(N)}/C^{\mr{grb}}_{\alpha}
, to
\mr{SU(N)}
. In particular,
\mu(b_{c_{\alpha}})
can be identified with the ’t Hooft flux of
\mr{SU(N)}_{c_{\alpha}}
factor of the gauge group. Now choose transition functions

t_{pq}^{\mr{S}}:=\left[\left(\widetilde{t}^{+}_{pq},\widetilde{t}^{-}_{pq},\widetilde{t}^{\mr{R}}_{pq},\widetilde{t}^{\mr{f}}_{pq},\widetilde{t}^{g}_{pq}\right)\right]:U_{pq}\longrightarrow G_{\mr{S}}~,

(362)

for the
G_{\mr{S}}
-bundle
P_{\mr{S}}\to{\mathbb{X}}
.
The cocycle on triple overlaps is given by

t_{pq}^{\mr{S}}t_{qr}^{\mr{S}}t_{rp}^{\mr{S}}=\left[\left(\zeta^{+}_{pqr},\zeta^{-}_{pqr},\zeta^{\mr{R}}_{pqr},\zeta^{\mr{f}}_{pqr},\zeta^{g}_{pqr}\right)\right]~.

(363)

Thus the cocycle condition is given by

\left(\zeta^{+}_{pqr},\zeta^{-}_{pqr},\zeta^{\mr{R}}_{pqr},\zeta^{\mr{f}}_{pqr},\zeta^{g}_{pqr}\right)\in C_{{\cal T}}^{\prime\prime}~.

(364)

As before, the cocycles
\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}
and
\zeta^{\mr{R}}_{pqr}
can be identified with the second Stiefel-Whitney class of
{\mathbb{X}}
and
P^{\mr{R}}
respectively:

\begin{split}w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})&=\left[\zeta_{pqr}^{\pm}\right]\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{X}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)~,\\
w_{2}\big(P^{\mr{R}}\big):&=\big[\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{R}}\big]\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{X}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right)~.\end{split}

(365)

The projections of the cocycles
\zeta^{\mr{f}}_{pqr}
and
\zeta^{g}_{pqr}
can be identified with representatives of the characteristic classes
\mu(b_{i,\mr{f}})
and
\mu(b_{\alpha})
by

\begin{split}\mu(b_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}})&=\big[p_{3,c_{i}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)\big]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},C_{c_{i}^{\mr{f}}})~,\\
\mu(b_{c_{\alpha}})&=\left[p_{4,c_{\alpha}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{g}\right)\right]\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},C_{c_{\alpha}})~.\end{split}

(366)

Finally, let
c_{1}(c_{i}^{\mr{s}})\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}})
be the first Chern class
of the
\mr{U(1)}
-bundle obtained by transfer of structure group of
P^{\mr{f},c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}
along the isomorphism (see footnote
23
)

\mr{U(1)}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mr{U(1)}~.

(367)

The cocycle
p_{3,c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{R}}\right)
represents the
\mathsf{\,mod\,}n_{i,\mr{s}}
reduction of
c_{1}(c_{i}^{\mr{s}})
:

\left[p_{3,c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{R}}\right)\right]=r_{n_{c_{i}^{\mr{s}}}}(c_{1}(c_{i}^{\mr{s}}))~.

(368)

We are now equipped to write the cohomological conditions. Due to the definition of
C_{{\cal T}}^{\prime\prime}
, we must have

\zeta^{-}_{pqr}=\zeta^{+}_{pqr}=\zeta^{\mr{R}}_{pqr}~,

(369)

which implies

w_{2}({\mathbb{X}})=w_{2}(P^{\mr{R}})~.

(370)

Next, if two trinions
T,T^{\prime}\in{\cal T}
are bounded by a common internal cutting curve
c_{T,\alpha}=c_{T^{\prime},\alpha}
, then we must have

p_{4,c_{T,\alpha}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{g}\right)=p_{4,c_{T^{\prime},\alpha}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{g}\right)~,

(371)

which implies the cohomological condition

\mu(b_{c_{T,\alpha}})=\mu(b_{c_{T^{\prime},\alpha}})~.

(372)

Next, we derive cohomological conditions for the two types of trinions.

\mr{fff}
Trinion.

For every trinion
T\in{\cal T}
of type
\mr{fff}
with two external cutting curves
c^{\mr{f}}_{T,i}
,
c^{\mr{f}}_{T,j}
and one internal cutting curve
c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\alpha}
, equations (
190
),(
193
) and the explicit form (
8.2
) implies that

\displaystyle\left(\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}\right)p_{3,c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)p_{3,c_{T,j}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)p_{4,c_{T,\alpha}^{g}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)

\displaystyle=1~,\quad N~~\text{even}~,

(373)

\displaystyle p_{3,c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)p_{3,c_{T,j}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)p_{4,c_{T,\alpha}^{g}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)

\displaystyle=1~,\quad N~~\text{odd}~.

(374)

This gives the cohomological condition

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\rho_{s}(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,j}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\alpha}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{[2,N]})~,\quad N~~\text{even}~,

(375)

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,j}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\alpha}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})~,\quad N~~\text{odd}~,

(376)

where
[2,N]=\mr{lcm}(2,N)
,
\rho_{s}
is the map in cohomology induced from the inclusion map
\rho_{s}:{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{[2,N]}
, and
\iota
is the map in cohomology obtained from inclusions in (
360
). If the trinion is bounded by two internal cutting curves
c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}},c_{T,\beta}^{\mr{f}}
and one external cutting curve
c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}
, the cohomological condition we get is

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\rho_{s}(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\beta}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{[2,N]})~,\quad N~~\text{even}~,

(377)

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\beta}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})~,\quad N~~\text{odd}~.

(378)

If the trinion is bounded by three internal cutting curves
c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}},c_{T,\beta}^{\mr{f}}
and
c_{T,\gamma}^{\mr{f}}
, the cohomological condition we get is

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\rho_{s}(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\beta}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\gamma}^{\mr{f}}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{[2,N]})~,\quad N~~\text{even}~,

(379)

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\beta}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\gamma}^{\mr{f}}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})~,\quad N~~\text{odd}~.

(380)

\mr{ffs}
Trinion.

For every trinion
T\in{\cal T}
of type
\mr{ffs}
with two external cutting curves
c^{\mr{f}}_{T,i},c^{\mr{s}}_{T,j}
and one internal cutting curve
c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\alpha}
, equations (
190
), (
193
) and the explicit form (
165
) implies that

\displaystyle\left(\zeta^{\pm}_{pqr}\right)\left(p_{3,c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)\right)^{-1}p_{4,c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{g}\right)p_{3,c_{T,j}^{\mr{s}}}\left(\zeta_{pqr}^{\mr{f}}\right)=1~.

(381)

This gives the cohomological condition

\displaystyle\rho_{s}(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}}))-\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,i}^{\mr{f}}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\alpha}}))+\iota\circ r_{n_{c_{T,j}^{\mr{s}}}}(c_{1}(c_{j}^{\mr{s}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{[2,N]})~,

(382)

where
r_{n}
denotes reduction
\mathsf{\,mod\,}~n
. If the trinion is bounded by two internal cutting curves
c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}},c_{T,\beta}^{\mr{f}}
and one external cutting curve
c_{T,i}^{\mr{s}}
, the cohomological condition we get is

\displaystyle\rho_{s}(w_{2}({\mathbb{X}}))+\iota(\mu(b_{c_{T,\alpha}^{\mr{f}}}))-\iota(\mu(b_{c^{\mr{f}}_{T,\beta}}))+\iota\circ r_{n_{c_{T,i}^{\mr{s}}}}(c_{1}(c_{T,i}^{\mr{s}}))

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{[2,N]})~.

(383)

Note that in this case, we cannot have all three internal cutting curves.
